English for Work · Health & life sciences

Cardiology English

Cardiology English for urgent handoffs, rhythm monitoring, echocardiography, risk discussions, procedural consent, heart-failure care, and rehabilitation.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For cardiologists, interventional cardiologists, electrophysiologists, cardiology fellows.

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Communicate a time-critical concern without assigning an unconfirmed diagnosis or delaying emergency care.
  • Explain what a rhythm recording can show and obtain a usable symptom account without promising detection.
  • Translate an imaging measurement into plain English without reducing the whole assessment to one number.
  • Separate symptom control from stroke prevention while explaining a benefit-and-bleeding discussion.
  • Explain a proposed procedure and clarify what has and has not been agreed.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.

01Giving an urgent cardiovascular handoffConcern first, uncertainty intact+

Your goal

Communicate a time-critical concern without assigning an unconfirmed diagnosis or delaying emergency care.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Concern first, uncertainty intact

Dr Vale hands over Chris, who reports chest pressure with breathlessness. The emergency response is already active. The cause, initial test results, and current measurements are not supplied in this exercise. Dr Chen is accepting the clinical handoff. The task is concise communication of reported symptoms, known actions, and unresolved information, not deciding whether chest discomfort is safe to wait.

Dr Vale: Referring physician / Dr Chen: Receiving cardiologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What symptoms are reported?

Answer and explanation

C. Chest pressure with breathlessness

The case supplies the symptom report without establishing its cause.

2. What is already active?

Answer and explanation

D. The emergency response

The emergency response begins before the communication exercise.

3. Who accepts the handoff?

Answer and explanation

C. Dr Chen

Dr Chen is the receiving cardiologist explicitly accepting the report.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Chest pressure and breathlessness are reported symptoms, not a confirmed infarction. Missing measurements must remain missing; the exercise cannot classify urgency or establish a diagnosis.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
acute coronary syndrome
Group of urgent conditions involving reduced coronary blood flow.evaluate suspected acute coronary syndrome
myocardial ischemia
Inadequate blood supply to heart muscle relative to its needs.assess myocardial ischemia
myocardial infarction
Heart-muscle injury and death caused by ischemia under diagnostic criteria.confirm myocardial infarction
retrosternal
Located behind the breastbone.describe retrosternal discomfort
exertional symptom
Symptom associated with physical effort.clarify exertional symptoms
rest symptom
Symptom occurring without physical exertion.report symptoms at rest
diaphoresis
Marked sweating, described in clinical context.report associated diaphoresis
dyspnea
Subjective sensation of difficult or uncomfortable breathing.describe reported dyspnea
troponin
Cardiac-injury biomarker interpreted with timing and clinical context.interpret a troponin result
serial testing
Repeated testing over a defined clinical sequence.explain serial testing
hemodynamic status
State of circulation assessed through relevant clinical information.communicate hemodynamic status
initial assessment
First clinical evaluation in the relevant encounter.report the initial assessment
prehospital report
Clinical information collected before arrival at the receiving hospital.relay the prehospital report
emergency activation
Initiation of the relevant emergency-response process.confirm emergency activation
symptom trajectory
Pattern of symptom change over time.describe the symptom trajectory
receiving team
Professionals accepting the next stage of care.identify the receiving team
time-critical concern
Concern requiring prompt action within the relevant clinical process.communicate a time-critical concern
closed-loop escalation
Urgent communication that includes confirmation of receipt and responsibility.complete closed-loop escalation
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Emergency activation and information transfer happen together. Do not wait for every detail or a language correction before following the real emergency process.

C Notice the language

Urgent does not mean speculative

Use a clear opening and closed confirmation. Do not increase certainty to sound decisive, and do not postpone an active emergency response until a report sounds polished.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the concern
I am handing over chest pressure with breathlessness.
State the action
The emergency response is already active.
Avoid invention
I do not have a verified measurement to report.
Separate diagnosis
The cause is not established in this handoff.
Request acceptance
Please confirm that your team is receiving the patient and report.
Preserve timing
I will distinguish reported onset from the time of our assessment.
Correct immediately
That detail needs correction before it is repeated.
Close the loop
I have your acknowledgment and the receiving contact.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Reports
Attributes symptoms to the patient rather than presenting an unverified finding as measured.
Already active
Identifies an emergency action that must not wait for this conversation to finish.
Confirm receiving
Requests acceptance instead of assuming that a sent message completed the transfer.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is strongest?

Answer and explanation

A. Chest pressure with breathlessness; the emergency response is active.

Naming the symptoms and active response communicates the actual concern and action without adding unsupported findings.

2. Which result must not be invented?

Answer and explanation

B. A normal troponin

No troponin result is supplied, so normal cannot be inserted as reassurance.

3. What completes the communication loop?

Answer and explanation

D. Confirmation of the receiving team and responsibility

Closed-loop communication requires acknowledgment rather than merely attempted contact.

4. What should not delay emergency care?

Answer and explanation

C. Completing every detail of this language exercise

The exercise is for language practice and must never replace or delay the real response.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Concern first, uncertainty intact

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Vale: Referring physician / Dr Chen: Receiving cardiologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • hemodynamic status
  • dyspnea
  • receiving team
  • Myocardial infarction
  • closed-loop escalation
  • symptom trajectory
  • Emergency activation
  • troponin
  • prehospital report
  • time-critical concern
  1. Dr Vale
    I am handing over Chris, who reports chest pressure with breathlessness. The emergency response is already active.
  2. Dr Chen
    I am receiving the report. Keep this 1 with the clinical team while we clarify the verified information.
  3. Dr Vale
    I will separate what Chris reports from what our team has actually observed or measured.
  4. Dr Chen
    Good. Report 2 as a symptom, and do not convert it into a diagnosis without the evidence.
  5. Dr Vale
    I do not yet have verified current measurements to pass on. I will update you as they become available.
  6. Dr Chen
    Then do not describe the 3 as normal. Missing information must stay visible while the real assessment continues.
  7. Dr Vale
    I also need to distinguish the patient's reported onset from the time someone first contacted the service.
  8. Dr Chen
    Yes. The 4 should retain its source and timing rather than be blended with later observations.
  9. Dr Vale
    I have not labeled this a confirmed infarction. The symptoms justify concern, but the cause is unestablished here.
  10. Dr Chen
    Correct. 5 is not a substitute label for every chest-pressure report.
  11. Dr Vale
    Nor should an absent test result be treated as a negative test that rules out the concern.
  12. Dr Chen
    Exactly. We are still awaiting the 6 result. Please include the sampling time when you pass it on.
  13. Dr Vale
    I will send any verified update through the active pathway rather than leave a separate routine message.
  14. Dr Chen
    Please do. The 7 needs to know which facts are new and who is responsible for them.
  15. Dr Vale
    Shall I wait until every field is complete before confirming transfer of the report?
  16. Dr Chen
    No. 8 is already underway; do not interrupt necessary care to perfect the wording.
  17. Dr Vale
    I will identify the unfinished information explicitly and confirm the contact for further clinical updates.
  18. Dr Chen
    That supports 9. I acknowledge the concern and am accepting this handoff through our clinical process.
  19. Dr Vale
    Understood. Reported symptoms, active response, unresolved cause, and no invented measurements or results.
  20. Dr Chen
    Correct. Continue communicating the 10 as verified information becomes available, alongside the real clinical response.

Answers and explanations
  1. time-critical concern Turn 2. A time-critical concern requires the active clinical response while the handoff conveys known facts and unresolved information.
  2. dyspnea Turn 4. Dyspnea is the reported breathing difficulty; the term does not by itself establish a cause or measurement.
  3. hemodynamic status Turn 6. Hemodynamic status requires relevant circulatory assessment and cannot be called normal from missing measurements.
  4. prehospital report Turn 8. The prehospital report contains information collected before hospital arrival and needs accurate source and timing attribution.
  5. Myocardial infarction Turn 10. Myocardial infarction is a diagnosis requiring appropriate evidence and is not established by the supplied symptom report alone.
  6. troponin Turn 12. Troponin is interpreted as a cardiac-injury biomarker with timing and context; no value is supplied in this case.
  7. receiving team Turn 14. The receiving team accepts the next stage of care and needs updates through the active clinical channel.
  8. Emergency activation Turn 16. Emergency activation initiates the relevant urgent response and must not be delayed while a language report is perfected.
  9. closed-loop escalation Turn 18. Closed-loop escalation includes acknowledgment and accepted responsibility, not simply sending information without confirmation.
  10. symptom trajectory Turn 20. Symptom trajectory describes how symptoms change over time and should be reported accurately as new information becomes available.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Vale: I am handing over Chris, who reports chest pressure with breathlessness. The emergency response is already active.
  2. Dr Chen: I am receiving the report. Keep this time-critical concern with the clinical team while we clarify the verified information.
  3. Dr Vale: I will separate what Chris reports from what our team has actually observed or measured.
  4. Dr Chen: Good. Report dyspnea as a symptom, and do not convert it into a diagnosis without the evidence.
  5. Dr Vale: I do not yet have verified current measurements to pass on. I will update you as they become available.
  6. Dr Chen: Then do not describe the hemodynamic status as normal. Missing information must stay visible while the real assessment continues.
  7. Dr Vale: I also need to distinguish the patient's reported onset from the time someone first contacted the service.
  8. Dr Chen: Yes. The prehospital report should retain its source and timing rather than be blended with later observations.
  9. Dr Vale: I have not labeled this a confirmed infarction. The symptoms justify concern, but the cause is unestablished here.
  10. Dr Chen: Correct. Myocardial infarction is not a substitute label for every chest-pressure report.
  11. Dr Vale: Nor should an absent test result be treated as a negative test that rules out the concern.
  12. Dr Chen: Exactly. We are still awaiting the troponin result. Please include the sampling time when you pass it on.
  13. Dr Vale: I will send any verified update through the active pathway rather than leave a separate routine message.
  14. Dr Chen: Please do. The receiving team needs to know which facts are new and who is responsible for them.
  15. Dr Vale: Shall I wait until every field is complete before confirming transfer of the report?
  16. Dr Chen: No. Emergency activation is already underway; do not interrupt necessary care to perfect the wording.
  17. Dr Vale: I will identify the unfinished information explicitly and confirm the contact for further clinical updates.
  18. Dr Chen: That supports closed-loop escalation. I acknowledge the concern and am accepting this handoff through our clinical process.
  19. Dr Vale: Understood. Reported symptoms, active response, unresolved cause, and no invented measurements or results.
  20. Dr Chen: Correct. Continue communicating the symptom trajectory as verified information becomes available, alongside the real clinical response.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A symptom time and a call time differ

A clinician corrects an urgent handoff while care continues.

  1. Referrer: The patient clarified that the pressure began before the call, not at the time recorded for contact.
  2. Cardiologist: Please preserve the corrected symptom onset separately from the call time while the response continues.
  3. Referrer: I have not assigned an exact minute because the patient cannot confirm one.
  4. Cardiologist: That is appropriate. An approximate report should not become a falsely precise timestamp.
  5. Referrer: The emergency pathway remains active, and the team has acknowledged the update.
  6. Cardiologist: Good. A corrected history should not interrupt the actual urgent clinical process.
  7. Referrer: I will identify which earlier entry is superseded through the approved record process.
  8. Cardiologist: Please also retain the source of the correction rather than present it as a new measured finding.
  9. Referrer: The handoff will distinguish onset, contact, and the time of the clinical assessment.
  10. Cardiologist: That gives the receiving team a usable sequence without inventing evidence.
An absent result is not negative

Two clinicians clarify an unavailable test result.

  1. Cardiologist: Do we have an actual verified result, or is the test still pending?
  2. Referrer: It is pending. The earlier summary said negative, but no result supports that word.
  3. Cardiologist: That needs prompt correction. An unavailable result cannot be treated as a negative finding.
  4. Referrer: I will correct the message and confirm that the receiving team acknowledges the change.
  5. Cardiologist: Please keep the current clinical concern and active response visible while the record is clarified.
  6. Referrer: I will not infer a safe delay from the missing result.
  7. Cardiologist: Correct. The actual assessment and urgent process determine the response.
  8. Referrer: The revised handoff will state pending rather than substitute a reassuring conclusion.
  9. Cardiologist: It should also identify the responsible clinician for reviewing the result when available.
  10. Referrer: Then the correction addresses both the inaccurate word and the unfinished follow-up responsibility.
A concise opening without a guessed diagnosis

A referrer practices the order of an urgent report.

  1. Referrer: I am handing over current chest pressure with breathlessness, and the emergency response is already active.
  2. Cardiologist: That opening gives the concern and action. What verified information is available next?
  3. Referrer: I can provide the patient account, but I do not have confirmed measurements in this report.
  4. Cardiologist: Please state that limitation rather than fill the gap with normal or stable.
  5. Referrer: Should I begin with the patient's entire remote medical history?
  6. Cardiologist: Lead with the current priority, then include the relevant background without delaying care.
  7. Referrer: I also need to confirm which team is accepting responsibility for the transfer.
  8. Cardiologist: Yes. A sent message alone does not establish an accepted handoff.
  9. Referrer: I will preserve the unresolved cause rather than use a diagnosis to sound decisive.
  10. Cardiologist: That is clearer and more accurate while the actual clinical assessment continues.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Concern first, uncertainty intact
  1. Read the corrected urgent handoff. Lead with the symptom report and emergency action.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the acceptance and missing-information statements without inserting measurements or a diagnosis.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A corrected onset time

During an active emergency handoff, the patient clarifies that symptoms began at 09:10, not 09:40. The receiving clinician acknowledges the correction. No cause is established.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Referrer: "The corrected reported onset is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 09:10

The patient corrects the reported onset to 09:10 in the supplied facts.

2. Receiver: "The earlier time of ___ is superseded."

Answer and explanation

B. 09:40

The earlier report is corrected rather than retained as the current onset.

3. Referrer: "The emergency response remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. active

Correcting the history does not interrupt the active emergency response.

4. Receiver: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unestablished

The timing correction does not itself establish a diagnosis.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Giving an urgent cardiovascular handoff
Original fictional case: Dr Vale hands over Chris, who reports chest pressure with breathlessness. The emergency response is already active. The cause, initial test results, and current measurements are not supplied in this exercise. Dr Chen is accepting the clinical handoff. The task is concise communication of reported symptoms, known actions, and unresolved information, not deciding whether chest discomfort is safe to wait.
Language workshop: Concern first, uncertainty intact
Communication goal: Communicate a time-critical concern without assigning an unconfirmed diagnosis or delaying emergency care.
Language guidance: Chest pressure and breathlessness are reported symptoms, not a confirmed infarction. Missing measurements must remain missing; the exercise cannot classify urgency or establish a diagnosis.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am handing over chest pressure with breathlessness. / The emergency response is already active. / I do not have a verified measurement to report. / The cause is not established in this handoff. / Please confirm that your team is receiving the patient and report. / I will distinguish reported onset from the time of our assessment. / That detail needs correction before it is repeated. / I have your acknowledgment and the receiving contact. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: acute coronary syndrome: Group of urgent conditions involving reduced coronary blood flow. / myocardial ischemia: Inadequate blood supply to heart muscle relative to its needs. / myocardial infarction: Heart-muscle injury and death caused by ischemia under diagnostic criteria. / retrosternal: Located behind the breastbone. / exertional symptom: Symptom associated with physical effort. / rest symptom: Symptom occurring without physical exertion. / diaphoresis: Marked sweating, described in clinical context. / dyspnea: Subjective sensation of difficult or uncomfortable breathing. / troponin: Cardiac-injury biomarker interpreted with timing and clinical context. / serial testing: Repeated testing over a defined clinical sequence. / hemodynamic status: State of circulation assessed through relevant clinical information. / initial assessment: First clinical evaluation in the relevant encounter. / prehospital report: Clinical information collected before arrival at the receiving hospital. / emergency activation: Initiation of the relevant emergency-response process. / symptom trajectory: Pattern of symptom change over time. / receiving team: Professionals accepting the next stage of care. / time-critical concern: Concern requiring prompt action within the relevant clinical process. / closed-loop escalation: Urgent communication that includes confirmation of receipt and responsibility. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected urgent handoff. Lead with the symptom report and emergency action. Swap roles. Repeat the acceptance and missing-information statements without inserting measurements or a diagnosis. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Vale: Referring physician / Dr Chen: Receiving cardiologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: During an active emergency handoff, the patient clarifies that symptoms began at 09:10, not 09:40. The receiving clinician acknowledges the correction. No cause is established.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Vale: I will identify the unfinished information explicitly and confirm the contact for further clinical updates.
Dr Chen: That supports closed-loop escalation. I acknowledge the concern and am accepting this handoff through our clinical process.
Dr Vale: Understood. Reported symptoms, active response, unresolved cause, and no invented measurements or results.
Dr Chen: Correct. Continue communicating the symptom trajectory as verified information becomes available, alongside the real clinical response.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Vocabulary: acute coronary syndrome: Group of urgent conditions involving reduced coronary blood flow. / myocardial ischemia: Inadequate blood supply to heart muscle relative to its needs. / myocardial infarction: Heart-muscle injury and death caused by ischemia under diagnostic criteria. / retrosternal: Located behind the breastbone. / exertional symptom: Symptom associated with physical effort. / rest symptom: Symptom occurring without physical exertion. / diaphoresis: Marked sweating, described in clinical context. / dyspnea: Subjective sensation of difficult or uncomfortable breathing. / troponin: Cardiac-injury biomarker interpreted with timing and clinical context. / serial testing: Repeated testing over a defined clinical sequence. / hemodynamic status: State of circulation assessed through relevant clinical information. / initial assessment: First clinical evaluation in the relevant encounter. / prehospital report: Clinical information collected before arrival at the receiving hospital. / emergency activation: Initiation of the relevant emergency-response process. / symptom trajectory: Pattern of symptom change over time. / receiving team: Professionals accepting the next stage of care. / time-critical concern: Concern requiring prompt action within the relevant clinical process. / closed-loop escalation: Urgent communication that includes confirmation of receipt and responsibility. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected urgent handoff. Lead with the symptom report and emergency action. Swap roles. Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

02Explaining ambulatory rhythm monitoringA symptom marker is not a diagnosis+

Your goal

Explain what a rhythm recording can show and obtain a usable symptom account without promising detection.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A symptom marker is not a diagnosis

Ellis describes intermittent palpitations. Dr Mora proposes an ambulatory recording to compare symptoms with heart rhythm. No arrhythmia has been established in this case. Ellis will receive device-specific instructions from the service. A symptom diary and event marker may help interpretation; they do not themselves diagnose the sensation or guarantee that every episode will be captured.

Ellis: Patient / Dr Mora: Cardiologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the proposed purpose?

Answer and explanation

C. Compare symptoms with recorded rhythm

The proposed recording explores symptom-rhythm relationships without a guaranteed diagnosis.

2. What is not established?

Answer and explanation

A. An arrhythmia

The case supplies palpitations but no confirmed rhythm diagnosis.

3. Who provides equipment instructions?

Answer and explanation

A. The service using the actual device

Instructions must match the actual device and service, not assumptions in the exercise.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Palpitations are a symptom; arrhythmia is a rhythm finding. A marked event helps locate information but does not itself diagnose it.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
palpitations
Awareness of the heartbeat as unusual, forceful, fast, or irregular.describe palpitations
electrocardiogram
Recording of the heart's electrical activity.interpret an electrocardiogram
arrhythmia
Abnormality of heart rhythm.investigate a suspected arrhythmia
sinus rhythm
Rhythm originating from the heart's usual sinus-node pacemaker.identify sinus rhythm
ectopic beat
Beat arising outside the usual initiating site.describe an ectopic beat
ambulatory monitor
Device recording heart rhythm during everyday activity.fit an ambulatory monitor
event marker
Device feature marking a time for later review.use the event marker
symptom diary
Record of symptoms, times, and relevant activity.keep a symptom diary
recording period
Time during which the device collects data.confirm the recording period
symptom-rhythm correlation
Relationship between reported symptoms and rhythm at the relevant time.assess symptom-rhythm correlation
paroxysmal
Occurring in episodes with onset and offset.describe paroxysmal symptoms
bradycardia
Heart rate slower than expected in the relevant context.assess bradycardia
tachycardia
Heart rate faster than expected in the relevant context.assess tachycardia
conduction
Transmission of electrical impulses through the heart.evaluate cardiac conduction
electrode lead
Electrical recording connection used in acquiring a trace.check an electrode lead
signal artifact
Distortion in a recording not representing the true underlying signal.identify signal artifact
signal quality
Adequacy of the recorded signal for interpretation.assess signal quality
monitor return
Process of returning equipment or transmitting its recorded data.confirm monitor return
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A routine monitor is not a substitute for the service's urgent-care instructions. Do not advise waiting for a recording review when new concerning symptoms require actual assessment.

C Notice the language

Explain the purpose before the equipment

A patient needs to know what question the monitor addresses, not just how long it is worn. Keep emergency instructions separate from routine recording instructions.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Explain the purpose
We want to compare what you feel with the rhythm at that time.
Separate symptom and finding
Palpitations do not name a specific rhythm by themselves.
Limit the promise
The recording may not capture every episode.
Make the diary useful
Include the time, what you felt, and what you were doing.
Clarify the marker
The marker identifies a time for review, not a diagnosis.
Use the actual instructions
The service will explain this device's controls and restrictions.
Separate urgent care
Follow the urgent-care instructions rather than waiting for the monitor report.
Close the process
Let us confirm how the recording reaches the reviewing team.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
At that time
Connects the reported sensation with the corresponding recording interval.
May not capture
Expresses a limitation without implying that the recording is useless.
Rather than waiting
Separates an urgent-care route from routine data collection.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which distinction is correct?

Answer and explanation

A. Palpitations are a symptom; an arrhythmia is a rhythm abnormality.

The symptom and the electrical finding require correlation rather than automatic equivalence.

2. What makes a diary entry useful?

Answer and explanation

A. Time, sensation, and activity

The relevant details help connect a reported event with the corresponding recorded interval.

3. Which promise is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Every episode will definitely be captured.

A finite recording may not capture every intermittent symptom or produce a definitive explanation.

4. What must remain separate?

Answer and explanation

A. Urgent-care instructions and routine monitor review

Routine recording does not replace appropriate urgent assessment of concerning clinical changes.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

A symptom marker is not a diagnosis

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ellis: Patient / Dr Mora: Cardiologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • arrhythmia
  • palpitations
  • Signal artifact
  • event marker
  • monitor return
  • recording period
  • signal quality
  • ambulatory monitor
  • symptom-rhythm correlation
  • symptom diary
  1. Ellis
    My heart sometimes feels as though it skips and then thumps. Is that enough to name the rhythm problem?
  2. Dr Mora
    Those are 1, a symptom description. We need appropriate evidence before naming a particular rhythm.
  3. Ellis
    The episodes come and go. They never seem to happen at the exact moment someone checks me.
  4. Dr Mora
    An 2 can record during ordinary activity, helping us look for a relationship with the episodes.
  5. Ellis
    Does agreeing to the monitor mean you have already diagnosed an arrhythmia from my account?
  6. Dr Mora
    No. An 3 has not been established in this case. The recording is part of investigating the question.
  7. Ellis
    What should I record when I notice the feeling, besides pressing whatever button the device has?
  8. Dr Mora
    The service will explain the device. A 4 should include the time, what you feel, and what you are doing.
  9. Ellis
    And pressing a marker does not mean the device has instantly told me what caused the sensation?
  10. Dr Mora
    Correct. The 5 identifies a time to review; it is not itself a diagnosis or a promise of real-time assessment.
  11. Ellis
    I am worried I will wear it and nothing will happen during the recording period.
  12. Dr Mora
    That is a possible limitation. We are seeking 6, and the available recording may not capture every intermittent event.
  13. Ellis
    If the trace looks strange while I move, will every unusual line be treated as a heart problem?
  14. Dr Mora
    No. 7 can affect recordings, so quality and context matter to the interpretation.
  15. Ellis
    Then I should follow the actual device instructions rather than borrow instructions from a friend's different monitor.
  16. Dr Mora
    Yes. Those instructions help protect 8 and explain the controls, restrictions, and reporting process for this device.
  17. Ellis
    If I develop a concerning new symptom, I should not assume pressing the marker replaces asking for help.
  18. Dr Mora
    Exactly. Follow the actual urgent-care instructions; the 9 is not permission to delay needed clinical assessment.
  19. Ellis
    I also want to know how you receive the recording and when the team explains its findings.
  20. Dr Mora
    We will confirm the 10 process and the service's review arrangements, without promising a result the data may not establish.

Answers and explanations
  1. palpitations Turn 2. Palpitations describe the awareness of an unusual heartbeat and do not by themselves identify a specific rhythm abnormality.
  2. ambulatory monitor Turn 4. An ambulatory monitor records rhythm during everyday activity and may help investigate symptoms that are intermittent.
  3. arrhythmia Turn 6. An arrhythmia is an abnormal heart rhythm, which has not been established by this symptom account.
  4. symptom diary Turn 8. A symptom diary records the sensation, timing, and activity so the reviewing clinician can interpret events in context.
  5. event marker Turn 10. An event marker identifies a time for subsequent review and does not itself diagnose the event.
  6. symptom-rhythm correlation Turn 12. Symptom-rhythm correlation compares the reported experience with the recorded rhythm at the relevant time.
  7. Signal artifact Turn 14. Signal artifact is distortion that may arise during recording and must be distinguished from the underlying cardiac signal.
  8. signal quality Turn 16. Signal quality concerns whether the recording is adequate for interpretation and depends on the actual equipment and circumstances.
  9. recording period Turn 18. The recording period is the data-collection interval and is not a reason to defer necessary urgent clinical assessment.
  10. monitor return Turn 20. Monitor return is the equipment or data-return process needed for review and should not be left as an assumed final step.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ellis: My heart sometimes feels as though it skips and then thumps. Is that enough to name the rhythm problem?
  2. Dr Mora: Those are palpitations, a symptom description. We need appropriate evidence before naming a particular rhythm.
  3. Ellis: The episodes come and go. They never seem to happen at the exact moment someone checks me.
  4. Dr Mora: An ambulatory monitor can record during ordinary activity, helping us look for a relationship with the episodes.
  5. Ellis: Does agreeing to the monitor mean you have already diagnosed an arrhythmia from my account?
  6. Dr Mora: No. An arrhythmia has not been established in this case. The recording is part of investigating the question.
  7. Ellis: What should I record when I notice the feeling, besides pressing whatever button the device has?
  8. Dr Mora: The service will explain the device. A symptom diary should include the time, what you feel, and what you are doing.
  9. Ellis: And pressing a marker does not mean the device has instantly told me what caused the sensation?
  10. Dr Mora: Correct. The event marker identifies a time to review; it is not itself a diagnosis or a promise of real-time assessment.
  11. Ellis: I am worried I will wear it and nothing will happen during the recording period.
  12. Dr Mora: That is a possible limitation. We are seeking symptom-rhythm correlation, and the available recording may not capture every intermittent event.
  13. Ellis: If the trace looks strange while I move, will every unusual line be treated as a heart problem?
  14. Dr Mora: No. Signal artifact can affect recordings, so quality and context matter to the interpretation.
  15. Ellis: Then I should follow the actual device instructions rather than borrow instructions from a friend's different monitor.
  16. Dr Mora: Yes. Those instructions help protect signal quality and explain the controls, restrictions, and reporting process for this device.
  17. Ellis: If I develop a concerning new symptom, I should not assume pressing the marker replaces asking for help.
  18. Dr Mora: Exactly. Follow the actual urgent-care instructions; the recording period is not permission to delay needed clinical assessment.
  19. Ellis: I also want to know how you receive the recording and when the team explains its findings.
  20. Dr Mora: We will confirm the monitor return process and the service's review arrangements, without promising a result the data may not establish.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A symptom diary needs a usable time

A clinician clarifies an ambiguous diary entry.

  1. Patient: My diary says the episode happened at seven, but I did not write morning or evening.
  2. Cardiologist: We need to clarify that before matching the report with the recording.
  3. Patient: I thought the device marker would automatically tell you everything about how I felt.
  4. Cardiologist: The marker identifies a time; your description adds the sensation and relevant activity.
  5. Patient: Could I describe the event without deciding that it was an arrhythmia?
  6. Cardiologist: Yes. A symptom account and the interpreted rhythm are different kinds of information.
  7. Patient: I remember the activity clearly, but I am less certain about the exact minute.
  8. Cardiologist: Keep that uncertainty visible rather than invent a precise time.
  9. Patient: Then the diary helps the review without becoming a diagnosis by itself.
  10. Cardiologist: Exactly. We compare the actual recording with the best accurate account you can provide.
A marker is not an emergency call

A patient checks the device's role.

  1. Patient: If I press the event marker during a concerning symptom, does that contact emergency help?
  2. Monitor clinician: You must not assume that. The marker's function and the service's actual arrangements need explanation.
  3. Patient: I thought someone might be watching every marked event immediately.
  4. Monitor clinician: The team should explain the real monitoring and review process, including its limits.
  5. Patient: Then urgent symptoms need the actual urgent-care route rather than waiting for a routine report.
  6. Monitor clinician: Correct. Follow the real clinical instructions supplied for your situation.
  7. Patient: Could you show me the device-specific instructions rather than use a generic explanation?
  8. Monitor clinician: Yes. Controls, restrictions, and transmission arrangements can differ between devices.
  9. Patient: I also want to know whom to contact if the equipment stops recording.
  10. Monitor clinician: We will distinguish the technical support route from urgent clinical assessment.
An artifact needs technical review

Two clinicians discuss an uncertain segment of a recording.

  1. Reviewing clinician: This segment contains signal distortion, and I do not want it described as a confirmed rhythm abnormality.
  2. Cardiologist: Please identify the limitation and the relevant recording interval for the actual review.
  3. Reviewing clinician: The patient's diary mentions movement around the same time, but that does not settle the cause.
  4. Cardiologist: Agreed. Context can help without replacing assessment of the trace itself.
  5. Reviewing clinician: I will distinguish a possible artifact from an established clinical finding.
  6. Cardiologist: That distinction should remain visible when we explain the result to the patient.
  7. Reviewing clinician: Does an uninterpretable segment prove the rhythm was normal during that period?
  8. Cardiologist: No. Missing usable information cannot establish either a normal or abnormal rhythm.
  9. Reviewing clinician: We should explain what the recording can support and any important limitation.
  10. Cardiologist: Exactly. A clear report does not hide uncertainty behind a confident-sounding label.

A symptom marker is not a diagnosis

  2. Swap roles. Repeat the device-instruction and urgent-care distinctions without inventing equipment settings.

Answer and explanation

Answer and explanation

Answer and explanation

The scenario provides a data clarification, not a diagnosed rhythm.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Explaining an echocardiogramA percentage is not the amount of heart left+

Your goal

Translate an imaging measurement into plain English without reducing the whole assessment to one number.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A percentage is not the amount of heart left

Ren reads an echocardiogram report containing a left-ventricular ejection-fraction measurement. No numerical value or heart-failure diagnosis is supplied. Ren thinks the percentage describes how much heart muscle is still alive. Dr Kim explains what the measurement represents and why symptoms, structure, filling, valves, and other findings also matter.

Ren: Patient / Dr Kim: Cardiologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Ren misunderstand?

Answer and explanation

D. The meaning of the ejection-fraction percentage

Ren thinks the percentage measures remaining living heart muscle, which is not what it describes.

2. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A numerical value or heart-failure diagnosis

The exercise provides a terminology discussion without an individual value or diagnosis.

3. What else belongs in interpretation?

Answer and explanation

C. Symptoms and other cardiac findings

The brief explicitly places the measurement within a broader clinical and imaging assessment.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Ejection fraction is a pumping proportion, not a percentage of living heart tissue or life expectancy. It is not the whole assessment of cardiac function.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
echocardiogram
Ultrasound examination of heart structure and function.explain an echocardiogram
ejection fraction
Proportion of ventricular blood volume expelled with a contraction.explain left-ventricular ejection fraction
systole
Phase of the cardiac cycle involving ventricular contraction and ejection.describe systolic function
diastole
Phase involving ventricular relaxation and filling.describe diastolic filling
left ventricle
Heart chamber pumping blood into the systemic circulation.assess the left ventricle
right ventricle
Heart chamber pumping blood toward the lungs.assess the right ventricle
stroke volume
Amount of blood ejected by a ventricle in one beat.distinguish stroke volume
preserved ejection fraction
Ejection fraction within the relevant preserved category despite possible other dysfunction.discuss preserved ejection fraction
reduced ejection fraction
Ejection fraction below the relevant category threshold in context.explain reduced ejection fraction
valvular regurgitation
Backward flow through a valve that does not close adequately.assess valvular regurgitation
valvular stenosis
Narrowing that restricts flow through a heart valve.explain valvular stenosis
wall motion
Movement of heart-muscle segments during the cardiac cycle.assess regional wall motion
diastolic function
Ventricular relaxation and filling characteristics.assess diastolic function
structural heart disease
Abnormality of heart anatomy or associated structures.investigate structural heart disease
ventricular volume
Amount of blood within a ventricle at a specified phase.measure ventricular volume
pressure estimate
Indirect approximation of pressure from available measurements.qualify a pressure estimate
image quality
Adequacy of images for the intended interpretation.describe image quality
comparison study
Earlier examination used to evaluate change.review a comparison study
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Preserved ejection fraction does not, by itself, exclude heart failure. Interpret the actual measurement with the clinical context and other findings; this case supplies no diagnostic category.

C Notice the language

Ask what the number means to the listener

A percentage can sound intuitive while being misunderstood. Establish the patient's interpretation before adding ranges or categories that may create a second misunderstanding.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Check the interpretation
What did that percentage suggest to you?
Define the measurement
It describes the proportion of blood pushed out with a contraction.
Correct gently
It is not the percentage of your heart that is still alive.
Name the chamber
This report refers to the left ventricle.
Broaden the picture
Filling, valves, symptoms, and other findings also matter.
Qualify the image
The report should state any important image-quality limitation.
Avoid a single-number diagnosis
We cannot interpret the whole situation from one number alone.
Separate a comparison
A change needs a comparable earlier study and clinical context.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Proportion versus amount
Ejection fraction is a proportion, whereas stroke volume is an amount per beat.
Not the percentage of
Corrects exactly what the number does not measure.
By itself
Limits the conclusion supported by one measurement without dismissing its usefulness.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What does ejection fraction measure?

Answer and explanation

C. A proportion of ventricular blood expelled with contraction

The measurement concerns pumping from a ventricle, not tissue survival or a global health percentage.

2. Which statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Preserved ejection fraction alone does not exclude heart failure.

Heart failure and cardiac function require the full clinical and imaging context.

3. Which distinction is useful?

Answer and explanation

D. Stroke volume is an amount; ejection fraction is a proportion.

The terms describe related but different ways of expressing ventricular ejection.

4. Which conclusion exceeds this case?

Answer and explanation

C. Ren has a specific heart-failure category.

The scenario intentionally supplies neither an individual measurement nor a diagnosis.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A percentage is not the amount of heart left

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ren: Patient / Dr Kim: Cardiologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Stroke volume
  • Ejection fraction
  • left ventricle
  • Diastolic function
  • ventricular volume
  • comparison study
  • image quality
  • Systole
  • valvular regurgitation
  • Preserved ejection fraction
  1. Ren
    The report gives a percentage. I thought it meant that only that much of my heart is still alive.
  2. Dr Kim
    1 does not measure how much heart tissue remains alive. Let me explain what the number represents.
  3. Ren
    Please do. I have been thinking of it as a percentage score for the whole heart.
  4. Dr Kim
    This measurement concerns the 2, the chamber that pumps blood toward the body.
  5. Ren
    What is the percentage actually a percentage of, if it is not the amount of working muscle?
  6. Dr Kim
    It relates the blood expelled to the 3 before that contraction. It describes a pumping proportion.
  7. Ren
    Then a report using an amount of blood per beat would be describing something related but different?
  8. Dr Kim
    Yes. 4 is an amount per beat, while ejection fraction expresses a proportion.
  9. Ren
    I also saw systolic and diastolic in the report. Are those two different parts of the heartbeat?
  10. Dr Kim
    5 involves contraction and ejection. Diastole involves relaxation and filling; both are relevant to function.
  11. Ren
    So the part about filling is not just an extra technical detail that can be ignored?
  12. Dr Kim
    Correct. 6 is part of the assessment, along with structure, valves, symptoms, and other findings.
  13. Ren
    If someone has a preserved percentage, does that prove they cannot have heart failure?
  14. Dr Kim
    No. 7 alone does not exclude it. We need the complete clinical picture, not just that label.
  15. Ren
    I understand. The report also discusses a valve, which I assumed the percentage already covered.
  16. Dr Kim
    Valve findings are assessed separately. For example, 8 describes backward flow through a valve, not the ejection fraction itself.
  17. Ren
    What if part of the scan was hard to see clearly? Should that limitation be explained too?
  18. Dr Kim
    Yes. Important 9 limitations should be stated rather than hidden behind a precise-looking number.
  19. Ren
    Then I should ask how this fits the rest of the assessment, not treat it as an isolated score.
  20. Dr Kim
    Exactly. Any 10 also needs context. We will explain the actual findings without inventing a diagnosis from one measurement.

Answers and explanations
  1. Ejection fraction Turn 2. Ejection fraction is the proportion of ventricular blood expelled with a contraction, not the proportion of living heart tissue.
  2. left ventricle Turn 4. The left ventricle is the chamber pumping blood into the body's systemic circulation and is the chamber named in this report.
  3. ventricular volume Turn 6. Ventricular volume is the blood in the chamber at a specified phase and provides the relevant basis for the pumping proportion.
  4. Stroke volume Turn 8. Stroke volume is the amount ejected in one beat, which differs from expressing that ejection as a proportion.
  5. Systole Turn 10. Systole includes ventricular contraction and ejection and is distinct from the relaxation and filling phase.
  6. Diastolic function Turn 12. Diastolic function concerns relaxation and filling and can matter even when a pumping proportion appears preserved.
  7. Preserved ejection fraction Turn 14. Preserved ejection fraction describes a measurement category and does not by itself exclude heart failure or other dysfunction.
  8. valvular regurgitation Turn 16. Valvular regurgitation means backward flow through a valve and is not synonymous with an ejection-fraction measurement.
  9. image quality Turn 18. Image quality affects what an examination can establish and relevant limitations should remain visible in the interpretation.
  10. comparison study Turn 20. A comparison study is an earlier examination used to assess change and must be interpreted for comparability and context.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ren: The report gives a percentage. I thought it meant that only that much of my heart is still alive.
  2. Dr Kim: Ejection fraction does not measure how much heart tissue remains alive. Let me explain what the number represents.
  3. Ren: Please do. I have been thinking of it as a percentage score for the whole heart.
  4. Dr Kim: This measurement concerns the left ventricle, the chamber that pumps blood toward the body.
  5. Ren: What is the percentage actually a percentage of, if it is not the amount of working muscle?
  6. Dr Kim: It relates the blood expelled to the ventricular volume before that contraction. It describes a pumping proportion.
  7. Ren: Then a report using an amount of blood per beat would be describing something related but different?
  8. Dr Kim: Yes. Stroke volume is an amount per beat, while ejection fraction expresses a proportion.
  9. Ren: I also saw systolic and diastolic in the report. Are those two different parts of the heartbeat?
  10. Dr Kim: Systole involves contraction and ejection. Diastole involves relaxation and filling; both are relevant to function.
  11. Ren: So the part about filling is not just an extra technical detail that can be ignored?
  12. Dr Kim: Correct. Diastolic function is part of the assessment, along with structure, valves, symptoms, and other findings.
  13. Ren: If someone has a preserved percentage, does that prove they cannot have heart failure?
  14. Dr Kim: No. Preserved ejection fraction alone does not exclude it. We need the complete clinical picture, not just that label.
  15. Ren: I understand. The report also discusses a valve, which I assumed the percentage already covered.
  16. Dr Kim: Valve findings are assessed separately. For example, valvular regurgitation describes backward flow through a valve, not the ejection fraction itself.
  17. Ren: What if part of the scan was hard to see clearly? Should that limitation be explained too?
  18. Dr Kim: Yes. Important image quality limitations should be stated rather than hidden behind a precise-looking number.
  19. Ren: Then I should ask how this fits the rest of the assessment, not treat it as an isolated score.
  20. Dr Kim: Exactly. Any comparison study also needs context. We will explain the actual findings without inventing a diagnosis from one measurement.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A pumping percentage is not living tissue

A patient clarifies an echocardiogram term.

  1. Patient: I thought the ejection-fraction percentage meant how much of my heart was still alive.
  2. Cardiologist: It describes a pumping proportion, not a percentage of living heart muscle.
  3. Patient: Could you explain what is being compared in that proportion?
  4. Cardiologist: It relates the blood expelled by the ventricle to the relevant volume before contraction.
  5. Patient: Then an amount of blood per beat would be a different measurement.
  6. Cardiologist: Yes. Stroke volume is an amount, while ejection fraction is a proportion.
  7. Patient: Does that one proportion describe every aspect of how the heart works?
  8. Cardiologist: No. Filling, valves, structure, symptoms, and other findings also need consideration.
  9. Patient: I would like the actual report explained without treating the number as a complete health score.
  10. Cardiologist: That is the right distinction: an important measurement, interpreted within the whole assessment.
Preserved does not mean every function is normal

A clinician corrects an overbroad inference.

  1. Cardiologist: You asked whether preserved ejection fraction excludes every possible heart problem.
  2. Patient: I assumed preserved meant the entire heart assessment was normal.
  3. Cardiologist: It describes that measurement category, not every aspect of cardiac structure or function.
  4. Patient: Does it also rule out heart failure by itself?
  5. Cardiologist: No. Heart failure requires the actual clinical assessment and may occur with preserved ejection fraction.
  6. Patient: Then the wording should not be shortened to nothing can be wrong.
  7. Cardiologist: Correct. We need to discuss what the report establishes and what it does not.
  8. Patient: I also want the valve and filling findings explained separately.
  9. Cardiologist: Those belong in the full interpretation rather than being absorbed into one percentage.
  10. Patient: That helps me understand why a reassuring-sounding label still needs context.
A comparison has an image-quality limitation

Two clinicians discuss a follow-up scan.

  1. Imaging clinician: The current examination has a relevant image-quality limitation that affects the comparison.
  2. Cardiologist: Please keep that visible rather than present the numerical difference as unquestionably meaningful.
  3. Imaging clinician: The earlier study used a different acquisition context, which also needs review.
  4. Cardiologist: Then we should establish comparability before describing a definite change in function.
  5. Imaging clinician: The report can state the actual measurements and the limitations of interpretation.
  6. Cardiologist: That gives the patient a clearer explanation than either ignoring the numbers or overstating them.
  7. Imaging clinician: Should we explain that a precise-looking value can still have uncertainty?
  8. Cardiologist: Yes. Numerical precision on a screen does not eliminate measurement limitations.
  9. Imaging clinician: I will identify what the comparison supports and what remains unresolved.
  10. Cardiologist: We can then discuss the actual clinical implications without manufacturing certainty from two numbers.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A percentage is not the amount of heart left
  1. Read the corrected echo explanation. Contrast proportion, amount, and living tissue.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the preserved-ejection-fraction explanation without assigning Ren a diagnosis.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A comparison needs context

A report cites an earlier scan, but the cardiologist has not yet reviewed whether the studies are comparable. No meaningful change has been established.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Patient: "The report refers to an earlier ___."

Answer and explanation

C. scan

An earlier examination is mentioned as a potential comparison.

2. Clinician: "I still need to review whether they are ___."

Answer and explanation

B. comparable

Comparability is unresolved and cannot be assumed from the existence of two reports.

3. Patient: "A meaningful change is not ___."

Answer and explanation

C. established

The case supplies no completed interpretation of a change.

4. Clinician: "We will interpret the findings in ___."

Answer and explanation

A. context

The relevant clinical and examination context must accompany the comparison.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Explaining an echocardiogram
Original fictional case: Ren reads an echocardiogram report containing a left-ventricular ejection-fraction measurement. No numerical value or heart-failure diagnosis is supplied. Ren thinks the percentage describes how much heart muscle is still alive. Dr Kim explains what the measurement represents and why symptoms, structure, filling, valves, and other findings also matter.
Language workshop: A percentage is not the amount of heart left
Communication goal: Translate an imaging measurement into plain English without reducing the whole assessment to one number.
Language guidance: Ejection fraction is a pumping proportion, not a percentage of living heart tissue or life expectancy. It is not the whole assessment of cardiac function.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What did that percentage suggest to you? / It describes the proportion of blood pushed out with a contraction. / It is not the percentage of your heart that is still alive. / This report refers to the left ventricle. / Filling, valves, symptoms, and other findings also matter. / The report should state any important image-quality limitation. / We cannot interpret the whole situation from one number alone. / A change needs a comparable earlier study and clinical context. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: echocardiogram: Ultrasound examination of heart structure and function. / ejection fraction: Proportion of ventricular blood volume expelled with a contraction. / systole: Phase of the cardiac cycle involving ventricular contraction and ejection. / diastole: Phase involving ventricular relaxation and filling. / left ventricle: Heart chamber pumping blood into the systemic circulation. / right ventricle: Heart chamber pumping blood toward the lungs. / stroke volume: Amount of blood ejected by a ventricle in one beat. / preserved ejection fraction: Ejection fraction within the relevant preserved category despite possible other dysfunction. / reduced ejection fraction: Ejection fraction below the relevant category threshold in context. / valvular regurgitation: Backward flow through a valve that does not close adequately. / valvular stenosis: Narrowing that restricts flow through a heart valve. / wall motion: Movement of heart-muscle segments during the cardiac cycle. / diastolic function: Ventricular relaxation and filling characteristics. / structural heart disease: Abnormality of heart anatomy or associated structures. / ventricular volume: Amount of blood within a ventricle at a specified phase. / pressure estimate: Indirect approximation of pressure from available measurements. / image quality: Adequacy of images for the intended interpretation. / comparison study: Earlier examination used to evaluate change. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected echo explanation. Contrast proportion, amount, and living tissue. Swap roles. Repeat the preserved-ejection-fraction explanation without assigning Ren a diagnosis. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Ren: Patient / Dr Kim: Cardiologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A report cites an earlier scan, but the cardiologist has not yet reviewed whether the studies are comparable. No meaningful change has been established.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ren: What if part of the scan was hard to see clearly? Should that limitation be explained too?
Dr Kim: Yes. Important image quality limitations should be stated rather than hidden behind a precise-looking number.
Ren: Then I should ask how this fits the rest of the assessment, not treat it as an isolated score.
Dr Kim: Exactly. Any comparison study also needs context. We will explain the actual findings without inventing a diagnosis from one measurement.
