Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Discussing atrial-fibrillation treatment goalsThe rhythm question and the stroke question+

Your goal

Separate symptom control from stroke prevention while explaining a benefit-and-bleeding discussion.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

The rhythm question and the stroke question

Avery has an established atrial-fibrillation diagnosis and asks why stroke prevention is discussed when the heartbeat feels calmer. Dr Patel will explain that symptom or rhythm management and stroke prevention address related but different questions. No risk score, medicine selection, dose, or instruction to stop treatment is supplied. Avery is worried about bleeding and wants that concern included.

Avery: Patient / Dr Patel: Cardiologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What diagnosis is established?

Answer and explanation

B. Atrial fibrillation

The brief explicitly identifies atrial fibrillation as an established diagnosis.

2. What does Avery ask about?

Answer and explanation

D. Why stroke prevention matters when symptoms feel calmer

Avery links improved sensations with a question about continuing risk discussion.

3. What is absent from the case?

Answer and explanation

B. A medicine selection or risk score

No individual score, prescription, dose, or stopping instruction may be invented.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Feeling fewer palpitations does not, by itself, settle the stroke-prevention decision. Do not derive an instruction to start or stop a medicine from this language exercise.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
atrial fibrillation
Irregular atrial electrical activity producing an irregular heart rhythm.explain atrial fibrillation
atrial flutter
Organized rapid atrial rhythm distinct from atrial fibrillation.distinguish atrial flutter
rate control
Strategy aimed at controlling heart rate.discuss rate control
rhythm control
Strategy aimed at restoring or maintaining an appropriate rhythm.discuss rhythm control
anticoagulation
Treatment reducing blood's ability to form clots through coagulation pathways.review anticoagulation
antiplatelet therapy
Treatment reducing platelet activity in clot formation.distinguish antiplatelet therapy
stroke risk
Likelihood of stroke in a defined clinical context and period.assess stroke risk
bleeding risk
Likelihood of bleeding under relevant clinical circumstances.assess bleeding risk
thromboembolism
Obstruction caused by a clot traveling from another site.discuss thromboembolism
cardioversion
Intervention intended to restore an appropriate heart rhythm.explain cardioversion
catheter ablation
Procedure targeting tissue involved in an abnormal rhythm.discuss catheter ablation
left atrial appendage
Pouch-like structure attached to the left atrium.explain the left atrial appendage
renal function
How effectively the kidneys perform their relevant functions.review renal function
drug interaction
Effect of one medicine or substance on another's action.check for a drug interaction
shared treatment decision
Care choice informed by evidence and the person's priorities.reach a shared treatment decision
bleeding history
Account of previous bleeding events and related circumstances.clarify the bleeding history
individual risk estimate
Context-dependent estimate for a person's likelihood of an outcome.explain an individual risk estimate
anticoagulation review
Clinical reassessment of anticoagulant use and relevant risks.arrange an anticoagulation review
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Anticoagulants and antiplatelets are not interchangeable labels. Explain their roles accurately within the individual clinical discussion rather than treating all "blood thinners" as identical.

C Notice the language

Explain why two goals remain on the agenda

Feeling better can be mistaken for proof that every risk has disappeared. Acknowledge the improvement while explaining the separate question and taking bleeding concerns seriously.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Separate the goals
Calming symptoms and preventing stroke are related but different goals.
Acknowledge improvement
It is useful to hear that the heartbeat feels calmer.
Keep assessment individual
We need to assess your stroke and bleeding risks in context.
Invite the concern
What worries you most about bleeding?
Clarify a colloquial term
Blood thinner can refer to medicines with different actions.
Avoid an instruction
This explanation is not a direction to stop your medicine.
Explore the history
Tell me about any previous bleeding and the circumstances.
Agree on review
Let us review the relevant information before settling the plan.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Related but different
Connects the goals without treating one as a substitute for the other.
By itself
Limits the significance of improved symptoms for a separate risk decision.
Before settling
Keeps the choice open until the required individual review is complete.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which distinction is correct?

Answer and explanation

A. Rhythm or symptom control does not replace the stroke-prevention assessment.

Treatment goals overlap but require their own relevant clinical assessment.

2. Which question includes Avery's concern?

Answer and explanation

A. What previous bleeding have you experienced, and what worries you?

Asking about previous bleeding and worries elicits relevant history without minimizing the concern.

3. Which instruction cannot be supplied here?

Answer and explanation

A. Stop anticoagulation because the heartbeat feels calmer.

The case gives no medication-change instruction or evidence for making one.

4. Why clarify blood thinner?

Answer and explanation

D. The informal phrase can hide different medicine actions.

The phrase is colloquial and does not distinguish anticoagulants from other therapies.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

The rhythm question and the stroke question

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Avery: Patient / Dr Patel: Cardiologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • atrial fibrillation
  • bleeding risk
  • individual risk estimate
  • bleeding history
  • stroke risk
  • Rate control
  • shared treatment decision
  • drug interaction
  • anticoagulation review
  • Anticoagulation
  1. Avery
    My heartbeat feels calmer now. Why are we still discussing stroke prevention as though nothing has changed?
  2. Dr Patel
    With 1, symptom improvement and stroke prevention are related but different parts of care.
  3. Avery
    I assumed that if I felt fewer episodes, the risk question would disappear with the symptoms.
  4. Dr Patel
    Feeling better is valuable, but 2 cannot be judged from how noticeable the heartbeat feels alone.
  5. Avery
    Please explain the terms rate control and rhythm control. They sound as though they mean exactly the same thing.
  6. Dr Patel
    3 concerns the heart rate; rhythm control concerns the rhythm itself. Neither phrase alone settles the prevention decision.
  7. Avery
    I would like the bleeding concern discussed as carefully as the benefit, not treated as a reason to stop asking.
  8. Dr Patel
    We should assess 4 and your concerns alongside the possible benefit of a proposed option.
  9. Avery
    I have called all these medicines blood thinners, but perhaps that hides an important difference.
  10. Dr Patel
    Yes. 5 and antiplatelet treatment have different actions. We should use the actual medicine name and purpose.
  11. Avery
    I had bleeding in the past, but I am not sure which details are relevant for this discussion.
  12. Dr Patel
    Tell me what happened and when. Your 6 deserves a careful account rather than an unexplained yes-or-no label.
  13. Avery
    Will other medicines and health conditions also affect how you judge the options for me?
  14. Dr Patel
    Yes. A 7, kidney function, and other relevant factors may need review in the actual assessment.
  15. Avery
    Then a general chart online cannot tell me precisely which treatment I should take or at what dose.
  16. Dr Patel
    Correct. An 8 needs your full history. We will review that together before discussing what the result means for you.
  17. Avery
    I also want to understand what matters most to me in choosing among appropriate options.
  18. Dr Patel
    That belongs in a 9, with accurate information about benefits, harms, and practical demands.
  19. Avery
    I understand this explanation does not mean I should stop a medicine because I feel better.
  20. Dr Patel
    Exactly. Any change requires the appropriate 10 and an individualized plan from your treating team.

Answers and explanations
  1. atrial fibrillation Turn 2. Atrial fibrillation is the established rhythm diagnosis here, and its care includes distinct symptom and stroke-risk questions.
  2. stroke risk Turn 4. Stroke risk requires individual assessment and cannot be assumed absent merely because palpitations feel less noticeable.
  3. Rate control Turn 6. Rate control aims at controlling heart rate and is distinct from a strategy focused on restoring or maintaining rhythm.
  4. bleeding risk Turn 8. Bleeding risk is a relevant part of the individualized benefit-and-harm discussion and should not be dismissed.
  5. Anticoagulation Turn 10. Anticoagulation acts on coagulation pathways and is not simply another name for every medicine informally called a blood thinner.
  6. bleeding history Turn 12. Bleeding history records previous events and their circumstances and is relevant to an individualized clinical review.
  7. drug interaction Turn 14. A drug interaction can change a medicine's effects and belongs in review of the actual medicines and clinical context.
  8. individual risk estimate Turn 16. An individual risk estimate depends on the relevant person, outcome, period, and clinical information, not a generic online chart alone.
  9. shared treatment decision Turn 18. A shared treatment decision combines clinical evidence with the person's informed priorities rather than ignoring either.
  10. anticoagulation review Turn 20. An anticoagulation review reassesses the actual treatment and risks; the language exercise supplies no authorization to change a medicine.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Avery: My heartbeat feels calmer now. Why are we still discussing stroke prevention as though nothing has changed?
  2. Dr Patel: With atrial fibrillation, symptom improvement and stroke prevention are related but different parts of care.
  3. Avery: I assumed that if I felt fewer episodes, the risk question would disappear with the symptoms.
  4. Dr Patel: Feeling better is valuable, but stroke risk cannot be judged from how noticeable the heartbeat feels alone.
  5. Avery: Please explain the terms rate control and rhythm control. They sound as though they mean exactly the same thing.
  6. Dr Patel: Rate control concerns the heart rate; rhythm control concerns the rhythm itself. Neither phrase alone settles the prevention decision.
  7. Avery: I would like the bleeding concern discussed as carefully as the benefit, not treated as a reason to stop asking.
  8. Dr Patel: We should assess bleeding risk and your concerns alongside the possible benefit of a proposed option.
  9. Avery: I have called all these medicines blood thinners, but perhaps that hides an important difference.
  10. Dr Patel: Yes. Anticoagulation and antiplatelet treatment have different actions. We should use the actual medicine name and purpose.
  11. Avery: I had bleeding in the past, but I am not sure which details are relevant for this discussion.
  12. Dr Patel: Tell me what happened and when. Your bleeding history deserves a careful account rather than an unexplained yes-or-no label.
  13. Avery: Will other medicines and health conditions also affect how you judge the options for me?
  14. Dr Patel: Yes. A drug interaction, kidney function, and other relevant factors may need review in the actual assessment.
  15. Avery: Then a general chart online cannot tell me precisely which treatment I should take or at what dose.
  16. Dr Patel: Correct. An individual risk estimate needs your full history. We will review that together before discussing what the result means for you.
  17. Avery: I also want to understand what matters most to me in choosing among appropriate options.
  18. Dr Patel: That belongs in a shared treatment decision, with accurate information about benefits, harms, and practical demands.
  19. Avery: I understand this explanation does not mean I should stop a medicine because I feel better.
  20. Dr Patel: Exactly. Any change requires the appropriate anticoagulation review and an individualized plan from your treating team.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A calmer heartbeat and stroke prevention

A patient separates two treatment goals.

  1. Patient: My heartbeat feels calmer, so I thought the stroke-prevention discussion was no longer relevant.
  2. Cardiologist: Symptom or rhythm management and stroke prevention address related but different questions.
  3. Patient: Could you explain the goals separately rather than assume I understand why each medicine is discussed?
  4. Cardiologist: Yes. We should use your actual clinical plan and explain what each part aims to address.
  5. Patient: Feeling better does not automatically establish that every risk has disappeared.
  6. Cardiologist: Correct. We must not infer a treatment change from the sensation alone.
  7. Patient: I want to discuss the actual benefits and harms before making any decision.
  8. Cardiologist: That belongs in an individualized assessment, not a universal rule from a symptom description.
  9. Patient: Please also make clear that this conversation is not an instruction to stop anything.
  10. Cardiologist: Any medicine decision must come from the responsible clinician and your verified plan.
Bleeding concern is not automatic refusal

A clinician invites a specific concern.

  1. Cardiologist: What worries you most about the proposed stroke-prevention discussion?
  2. Patient: I am concerned about bleeding, but I do not want that recorded as refusing every option.
  3. Cardiologist: A concern deserves assessment and explanation; it is not automatically an informed refusal.
  4. Patient: I would like the risk described with actual context rather than just the word small.
  5. Cardiologist: Where relevant figures are available, we should explain the outcome, timeframe, and uncertainty.
  6. Patient: Could my prior history change how the options are considered?
  7. Cardiologist: The actual clinical history matters and should be reviewed rather than assumed.
  8. Patient: I also need to distinguish a possible adverse effect from a certainty that it will happen.
  9. Cardiologist: We will explain both benefits and harms without promising zero risk.
  10. Patient: That gives me a basis for questions rather than pressure to agree before understanding.
Rate and rhythm are different aims

A patient asks about two common terms.

  1. Patient: I hear rate control and rhythm control used as though they mean the same thing.
  2. Cardiologist: They describe different aims, even though both may appear in an atrial-fibrillation discussion.
  3. Patient: Could you explain the distinction without selecting a treatment for me from the terminology alone?
  4. Cardiologist: Rate concerns how fast the heart beats; rhythm concerns the pattern or organization of the rhythm.
  5. Patient: Then a change in one does not automatically settle every question about the other.
  6. Cardiologist: Correct. The actual assessment determines the relevant strategy and its goals.
  7. Patient: I also understand that stroke prevention remains a separate discussion.
  8. Cardiologist: Yes. We should avoid presenting symptom control as a substitute for that assessment.
  9. Patient: Could the summary identify each goal so I can explain the plan accurately?
  10. Cardiologist: It should, using the actual agreed care rather than an invented medicine instruction.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The rhythm question and the stroke question
  1. Read the corrected atrial-fibrillation discussion. Contrast symptom control with stroke prevention.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the bleeding-history question and the explicit boundary against inferring a medicine change.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify which medicine is meant

Nico says blood thinner but cannot recall the product. The clinician will verify its name and purpose before discussing a change. No stop instruction has been given.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Nico: "I do not recall the product ___."

Answer and explanation

B. name

The medicine's identity remains unresolved despite the informal label.

2. Clinician: "We will verify its name and ___."

Answer and explanation

A. purpose

Verification must establish what the actual medicine is intended to do.

3. Nico: "No stop instruction has been ___."

Answer and explanation

B. given

The case provides no instruction to discontinue the medicine.

4. Clinician: "A change needs individual ___."

Answer and explanation

C. review

An individual clinical review is required before deciding on a change.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Discussing coronary angiography consentA diagnostic test is not automatic treatment+

Your goal

Explain a proposed procedure and clarify what has and has not been agreed.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

A diagnostic test is not automatic treatment

Dr Lewis discusses proposed diagnostic coronary angiography with Blair. Blair assumes a stent will definitely be inserted during the same visit. The exercise supplies no coronary anatomy, intervention decision, risk percentage, or preparation instructions. Dr Lewis must explain the diagnostic purpose, address questions, and clarify the actual consent arrangements for any possible intervention rather than treat attendance as agreement.

Blair: Patient / Dr Lewis: Cardiologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is proposed?

Answer and explanation

B. Diagnostic coronary angiography

The case proposes a diagnostic investigation, not a settled intervention.

2. What does Blair assume?

Answer and explanation

C. A stent will definitely be inserted

Blair confuses the proposed diagnostic procedure with an automatic treatment decision.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. Coronary anatomy or an intervention decision

The clinician cannot invent anatomical findings or a final intervention plan.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Diagnostic angiography and coronary intervention are different actions. Possible same-session treatment must be explained within the actual plan and consent process; it is not automatic.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
coronary angiography
Imaging of coronary arteries using an appropriate angiographic technique.explain coronary angiography
cardiac catheterization
Procedure using a catheter to investigate or treat cardiac conditions.discuss cardiac catheterization
catheter laboratory
Specialist environment for catheter-based cardiac procedures.attend the catheter laboratory
vascular access
Route of entry into a blood vessel for a procedure.explain vascular access
radial access
Vessel access through the radial artery.discuss radial access
femoral access
Vessel access through the femoral artery.discuss femoral access
contrast medium
Substance used to improve visibility of structures on imaging.explain contrast medium
coronary anatomy
Arrangement and features of the coronary arteries.assess coronary anatomy
coronary narrowing
Reduction in the internal opening of a coronary artery.evaluate coronary narrowing
percutaneous coronary intervention
Catheter-based procedure used to treat appropriate coronary artery disease.discuss percutaneous coronary intervention
stent
Device placed to help maintain an opening in a vessel or structure.explain a coronary stent
diagnostic procedure
Procedure intended to obtain information about a condition.distinguish a diagnostic procedure
procedural risk
Possible harm associated with a procedure.discuss procedural risk
sedation
Medicines used to reduce awareness, anxiety, or discomfort to an intended degree.explain the sedation plan
procedure consent
Agreement to a specified procedure after appropriate discussion.confirm procedure consent
access-site complication
Problem occurring at the vessel-entry location.explain access-site complications
postprocedure observation
Monitoring after a procedure under the actual care plan.explain postprocedure observation
preparation instruction
Direction needed before a procedure for the individual plan.verify preparation instructions
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Do not supply fasting, medicine-holding, access-site, or sedation instructions from a language exercise. Those require the actual team's individualized instructions.

C Notice the language

Explain before asking for agreement

A familiar procedure name may hide different expectations. Ask what the patient thinks will happen, then clarify the intended test and any separately discussed intervention.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Check expectations
What have you understood will happen during the visit?
State the diagnostic aim
The proposed test is intended to show the coronary arteries.
Separate treatment
A diagnostic test is not an automatic decision to insert a stent.
Clarify the agreement
We need to discuss exactly what you are consenting to.
Discuss risk accurately
We will explain the risks relevant to the actual procedure and your situation.
Invite alternatives
Please ask about reasonable alternatives and what they would establish.
Verify preparation
Use the instructions from the team doing your procedure.
Keep the decision open
No intervention decision is supplied by this conversation alone.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Intended to show
States a diagnostic purpose rather than a guaranteed finding.
Exactly what
Requests the scope of agreement instead of relying on a vague procedure label.
Possible versus planned
A possible intervention is not the same as a settled treatment plan.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which distinction is essential?

Answer and explanation

D. Diagnostic angiography does not automatically mean stent insertion.

The proposed diagnostic purpose and any intervention agreement must be distinguished.

2. Which instruction would be inappropriate to invent?

Answer and explanation

B. A medication-holding schedule

Preparation and medicine instructions must come from the actual clinical team.

3. What does informed agreement require here?

Answer and explanation

B. A clear discussion of the specified procedure and relevant options

Consent concerns an understood, specified proposal and cannot be replaced by attendance alone.

4. Which statement preserves the facts?

Answer and explanation

A. No intervention decision is established in this case.

The scenario does not provide findings or a decision authorizing intervention.

E Conversations

A diagnostic test is not automatic treatment

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Blair: Patient / Dr Lewis: Cardiologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • preparation instructions
  • vascular access
  • stent
  • postprocedure observation
  • Percutaneous coronary intervention
  • coronary angiography
  • procedure consent
  • contrast medium
  • diagnostic procedure
  • coronary anatomy
  1. Blair
    I told my family I am coming in to have a stent. Is that the right description of the appointment?
  2. Dr Lewis
    We are discussing 1, a diagnostic investigation. Let us clarify the difference before you make arrangements around an assumption.
  3. Blair
    I thought the test and the treatment were simply two names for what always happens together.
  4. Dr Lewis
    A 2 gathers information. Treatment may be discussed in relation to findings, but it is not automatic.
  5. Blair
    Then the purpose is to understand the arteries, rather than promise that a particular device will be inserted?
  6. Dr Lewis
    Yes. It helps assess 3. We need to explain how any findings would affect the options before you agree to proceed.
  7. Blair
    Please explain the phrase PCI if it comes up. I hear abbreviations and lose track of which action is meant.
  8. Dr Lewis
    4 is a catheter-based treatment approach. We should explain the actual proposal rather than hide it behind initials.
  9. Blair
    What would the consent discussion need to cover if treatment during the same visit were a possibility?
  10. Dr Lewis
    The 5 must be clear about the proposed actions, alternatives, relevant risks, and your questions.
  11. Blair
    I do not want signing for a test to be treated as agreement to something nobody has explained.
  12. Dr Lewis
    That is why we clarify scope. A 6 is not something we should describe as already decided when it has not been.
  13. Blair
    I have read different stories about the wrist and groin. Should I choose from those accounts myself?
  14. Dr Lewis
    The team will discuss the actual 7 plan and its implications with you. Do not infer your plan from another person's experience.
  15. Blair
    I also want to ask about the dye and any concerns in my own medical history.
  16. Dr Lewis
    We should review those concerns when explaining the 8 and the procedure's risks in your individual circumstances.
  17. Blair
    Should I follow preparation advice I found online while I wait for the written instructions?
  18. Dr Lewis
    Follow the team's 9. If another source gives different fasting or medicine advice, please contact us to clarify it before making a change.
  19. Blair
    I can explain the visit more accurately now: a diagnostic discussion, with any intervention requiring clarity.
  20. Dr Lewis
    Good. We will also explain 10 and practical arrangements, without promising an outcome or discharge time we have not established.

Answers and explanations
  1. coronary angiography Turn 2. Coronary angiography examines coronary arteries; it does not automatically include stent treatment.
  2. diagnostic procedure Turn 4. A diagnostic procedure obtains information and must be distinguished from a separate treatment action or decision.
  3. coronary anatomy Turn 6. Coronary anatomy describes the arteries; this case supplies no anatomical findings.
  4. Percutaneous coronary intervention Turn 8. Percutaneous coronary intervention is catheter-based treatment, distinct from diagnostic imaging alone.
  5. procedure consent Turn 10. Procedure consent concerns the specified actions agreed after discussion, including any possible intervention in the actual plan.
  6. stent Turn 12. A stent is a device used in some interventions and is not automatically inserted merely because angiography is discussed.
  7. vascular access Turn 14. Vascular access identifies the actual vessel-entry route, not a plan borrowed from another patient.
  8. contrast medium Turn 16. Contrast medium improves imaging visibility and its relevant risks and preparation need individual clinical review.
  9. preparation instructions Turn 18. Preparation instructions must match the real procedure and medicines; the language exercise does not supply a safe substitute.
  10. postprocedure observation Turn 20. Postprocedure observation means planned monitoring; it does not establish a guaranteed discharge time.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Blair: I told my family I am coming in to have a stent. Is that the right description of the appointment?
  2. Dr Lewis: We are discussing coronary angiography, a diagnostic investigation. Let us clarify the difference before you make arrangements around an assumption.
  3. Blair: I thought the test and the treatment were simply two names for what always happens together.
  4. Dr Lewis: A diagnostic procedure gathers information. Treatment may be discussed in relation to findings, but it is not automatic.
  5. Blair: Then the purpose is to understand the arteries, rather than promise that a particular device will be inserted?
  6. Dr Lewis: Yes. It helps assess coronary anatomy. We need to explain how any findings would affect the options before you agree to proceed.
  7. Blair: Please explain the phrase PCI if it comes up. I hear abbreviations and lose track of which action is meant.
  8. Dr Lewis: Percutaneous coronary intervention is a catheter-based treatment approach. We should explain the actual proposal rather than hide it behind initials.
  9. Blair: What would the consent discussion need to cover if treatment during the same visit were a possibility?
  10. Dr Lewis: The procedure consent must be clear about the proposed actions, alternatives, relevant risks, and your questions.
  11. Blair: I do not want signing for a test to be treated as agreement to something nobody has explained.
  12. Dr Lewis: That is why we clarify scope. A stent is not something we should describe as already decided when it has not been.
  13. Blair: I have read different stories about the wrist and groin. Should I choose from those accounts myself?
  14. Dr Lewis: The team will discuss the actual vascular access plan and its implications with you. Do not infer your plan from another person's experience.
  15. Blair: I also want to ask about the dye and any concerns in my own medical history.
  16. Dr Lewis: We should review those concerns when explaining the contrast medium and the procedure's risks in your individual circumstances.
  17. Blair: Should I follow preparation advice I found online while I wait for the written instructions?
  18. Dr Lewis: Follow the team's preparation instructions. If another source gives different fasting or medicine advice, please contact us to clarify it before making a change.
  19. Blair: I can explain the visit more accurately now: a diagnostic discussion, with any intervention requiring clarity.
  20. Dr Lewis: Good. We will also explain postprocedure observation and practical arrangements, without promising an outcome or discharge time we have not established.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An angiogram is not automatically a stent

A patient asks about procedural scope.

  1. Patient: I thought agreeing to an angiogram meant a stent would definitely be placed at the same time.
  2. Cardiologist: A diagnostic examination and a possible intervention are different parts of the discussion.
  3. Patient: Could you explain what is actually proposed and what decisions remain conditional?
  4. Cardiologist: Yes. The actual procedure plan, findings, options, and consent arrangements need to be clear.
  5. Patient: I do not want a possible next step presented as though it has already been selected.
  6. Cardiologist: That is important. We should distinguish what is planned from what might be considered.
  7. Patient: Will you explain the relevant benefits, risks, and alternatives using my actual circumstances?
  8. Cardiologist: That belongs in the appropriate consent discussion, not a generic promise.
  9. Patient: I also want to know whom to ask about preparation instructions that seem inconsistent.
  10. Cardiologist: We should verify them with the responsible service rather than ask you to guess.
Contrast questions need the actual history

A clinician clarifies an imprecise reaction report.

  1. Cardiologist: You mentioned a previous reaction around an imaging procedure. What do you remember happening?
  2. Patient: I remember feeling unwell, but I do not know which product was involved.
  3. Cardiologist: Let us preserve that uncertainty and review the available record rather than invent a substance.
  4. Patient: I was worried that saying allergy would automatically answer every preparation question.
  5. Cardiologist: The actual reaction history needs assessment; a label alone may not explain the event.
  6. Patient: I can describe the timing and symptoms, but not a confirmed cause.
  7. Cardiologist: Those details help, provided we distinguish your account from an established causal conclusion.
  8. Patient: Could the procedure team clarify the actual implications for the planned examination?
  9. Cardiologist: Yes. The relevant clinicians should review the real history and preparation requirements.
  10. Patient: Then I will not be asked to decide which precaution applies from a vague memory.
Understanding is not the same as agreement

A patient checks the consent conversation.

  1. Patient: I can repeat the explanation, but I still have a question before deciding about the procedure.
  2. Cardiologist: Understanding the information does not mean you have already agreed.
  3. Patient: I want to compare the actual alternatives, including their relevant limitations.
  4. Cardiologist: We should explain those in the context of the recommendation and your circumstances.
  5. Patient: A signed information sheet should not close the discussion if something remains unclear.
  6. Cardiologist: Correct. The consent process includes meaningful explanation and the appropriate decision.
  7. Patient: Could you clarify the difference between the expected benefit and a guaranteed outcome?
  8. Cardiologist: An expected benefit is an aim or supported possibility, not a promise of success.
  9. Patient: Then we can document what is understood and what decision has actually been made.
  10. Cardiologist: Yes. The record should not infer authorization from a nod or a completed form alone.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A diagnostic test is not automatic treatment
  1. Read the corrected procedure discussion. Contrast diagnostic information with an intervention decision.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the consent-scope question and the instruction to use the actual team's preparation advice.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A preparation sheet needs clarification

Morgan receives two different preparation sheets. The procedural team will verify which applies. Morgan has not been told to alter medicines in this scenario.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Morgan: "The two sheets give different ___."

Answer and explanation

B. instructions

The problem is conflicting preparation information rather than a completed clinical decision.

2. Clinician: "The procedural team will ___ which applies."

Answer and explanation

B. verify

Verification by the responsible team is the supplied next step.

3. Morgan: "No medicine change is ___ here."

Answer and explanation

A. authorized

The scenario supplies no authorization to alter a medicine.

4. Clinician: "The correct plan is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. clarified

Conflicting documents leave the relevant instruction unresolved until verification.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Professional audience: cardiologists, interventional cardiologists, electrophysiologists, cardiology fellows

Lesson 05: Discussing coronary angiography consent
Original fictional case: Dr Lewis discusses proposed diagnostic coronary angiography with Blair. Blair assumes a stent will definitely be inserted during the same visit. The exercise supplies no coronary anatomy, intervention decision, risk percentage, or preparation instructions. Dr Lewis must explain the diagnostic purpose, address questions, and clarify the actual consent arrangements for any possible intervention rather than treat attendance as agreement.
Language workshop: A diagnostic test is not automatic treatment
Communication goal: Explain a proposed procedure and clarify what has and has not been agreed.
Language guidance: Diagnostic angiography and coronary intervention are different actions. Possible same-session treatment must be explained within the actual plan and consent process; it is not automatic.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What have you understood will happen during the visit? / The proposed test is intended to show the coronary arteries. / A diagnostic test is not an automatic decision to insert a stent. / We need to discuss exactly what you are consenting to. / We will explain the risks relevant to the actual procedure and your situation. / Please ask about reasonable alternatives and what they would establish. / Use the instructions from the team doing your procedure. / No intervention decision is supplied by this conversation alone. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: coronary angiography: Imaging of coronary arteries using an appropriate angiographic technique. / cardiac catheterization: Procedure using a catheter to investigate or treat cardiac conditions. / catheter laboratory: Specialist environment for catheter-based cardiac procedures. / vascular access: Route of entry into a blood vessel for a procedure. / radial access: Vessel access through the radial artery. / femoral access: Vessel access through the femoral artery. / contrast medium: Substance used to improve visibility of structures on imaging. / coronary anatomy: Arrangement and features of the coronary arteries. / coronary narrowing: Reduction in the internal opening of a coronary artery. / percutaneous coronary intervention: Catheter-based procedure used to treat appropriate coronary artery disease. / stent: Device placed to help maintain an opening in a vessel or structure. / diagnostic procedure: Procedure intended to obtain information about a condition. / procedural risk: Possible harm associated with a procedure. / sedation: Medicines used to reduce awareness, anxiety, or discomfort to an intended degree. / procedure consent: Agreement to a specified procedure after appropriate discussion. / access-site complication: Problem occurring at the vessel-entry location. / postprocedure observation: Monitoring after a procedure under the actual care plan. / preparation instruction: Direction needed before a procedure for the individual plan. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 05: Discussing coronary angiography consent
Original fictional case: Dr Lewis discusses proposed diagnostic coronary angiography with Blair. Blair assumes a stent will definitely be inserted during the same visit. The exercise supplies no coronary anatomy, intervention decision, risk percentage, or preparation instructions. Dr Lewis must explain the diagnostic purpose, address questions, and clarify the actual consent arrangements for any possible intervention rather than treat attendance as agreement.
Language workshop: A diagnostic test is not automatic treatment
Communication goal: Explain a proposed procedure and clarify what has and has not been agreed.
Language guidance: Diagnostic angiography and coronary intervention are different actions. Possible same-session treatment must be explained within the actual plan and consent process; it is not automatic.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What have you understood will happen during the visit? / The proposed test is intended to show the coronary arteries. / A diagnostic test is not an automatic decision to insert a stent. / We need to discuss exactly what you are consenting to. / We will explain the risks relevant to the actual procedure and your situation. / Please ask about reasonable alternatives and what they would establish. / Use the instructions from the team doing your procedure. / No intervention decision is supplied by this conversation alone. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: coronary angiography: Imaging of coronary arteries using an appropriate angiographic technique. / cardiac catheterization: Procedure using a catheter to investigate or treat cardiac conditions. / catheter laboratory: Specialist environment for catheter-based cardiac procedures. / vascular access: Route of entry into a blood vessel for a procedure. / radial access: Vessel access through the radial artery. / femoral access: Vessel access through the femoral artery. / contrast medium: Substance used to improve visibility of structures on imaging. / coronary anatomy: Arrangement and features of the coronary arteries. / coronary narrowing: Reduction in the internal opening of a coronary artery. / percutaneous coronary intervention: Catheter-based procedure used to treat appropriate coronary artery disease. / stent: Device placed to help maintain an opening in a vessel or structure. / diagnostic procedure: Procedure intended to obtain information about a condition. / procedural risk: Possible harm associated with a procedure. / sedation: Medicines used to reduce awareness, anxiety, or discomfort to an intended degree. / procedure consent: Agreement to a specified procedure after appropriate discussion. / access-site complication: Problem occurring at the vessel-entry location. / postprocedure observation: Monitoring after a procedure under the actual care plan. / preparation instruction: Direction needed before a procedure for the individual plan. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected procedure discussion. Contrast diagnostic information with an intervention decision. Swap roles. Repeat the consent-scope question and the instruction to use the actual team's preparation advice. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Blair: Patient / Dr Lewis: Cardiologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Morgan receives two different preparation sheets. The procedural team will verify which applies. Morgan has not been told to alter medicines in this scenario.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Blair: Should I follow preparation advice I found online while I wait for the written instructions?
Dr Lewis: Follow the team's preparation instructions. If another source gives different fasting or medicine advice, please contact us to clarify it before making a change.
Blair: I can explain the visit more accurately now: a diagnostic discussion, with any intervention requiring clarity.
Dr Lewis: Good. We will also explain postprocedure observation and practical arrangements, without promising an outcome or discharge time we have not established.
END REFERENCE
06Checking a heart-failure home planTeach-back checks the explanation+

Your goal

Check understanding of an individualized plan without treating a printed sheet as proof of comprehension.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Teach-back checks the explanation

Dr Evans reviews an individualized heart-failure plan with Lee. The actual plan includes symptom monitoring, a verified medicine list, contact routes, and follow-up. No weight threshold, fluid limit, or dose adjustment is supplied here. Lee has received the sheet but is unclear which number is for routine questions and which route is for urgent concerns. The clinician must clarify the distinction and use teach-back without blame.

Dr Evans: Cardiologist / Lee: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has Lee received?

Answer and explanation

D. A written individualized plan

The sheet exists, but this exercise supplies no clinical thresholds or dosing rules.

2. What is unclear?

Answer and explanation

C. Routine versus urgent contact routes

Lee needs the two communication routes distinguished explicitly.

3. What does teach-back check?

Answer and explanation

B. The clarity of the explanation received

Teach-back reveals how the explanation was understood and supports clarification.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A written action plan does not prove understanding. Clarify the actual instructions; the lesson must not become a universal weight, fluid, or medicine-adjustment protocol.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
heart failure
Clinical syndrome in which cardiac dysfunction causes symptoms and signs in context.explain heart failure
congestion
Fluid accumulation associated with relevant circulatory dysfunction.assess congestion
edema
Swelling caused by fluid in tissues.describe peripheral edema
orthopnea
Breathlessness occurring when lying flat.ask about orthopnea
paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea
Episodes of breathlessness waking a person from sleep.clarify nocturnal dyspnea
exercise tolerance
Ability to perform physical activity without limiting symptoms.describe exercise tolerance
daily weight record
Log of body-weight measurements under an agreed monitoring plan.review a daily weight record
fluid balance
Relationship between fluid intake, output, and retention.assess fluid balance
fluid restriction
Individual instruction limiting fluid intake when clinically indicated.clarify a prescribed fluid restriction
sodium intake
Amount of sodium consumed through food and drink.discuss sodium intake
diuretic
Medicine increasing urine production.explain a prescribed diuretic
guideline-directed therapy
Treatment consistent with applicable evidence-based recommendations and individual circumstances.review guideline-directed therapy
decompensation
Worsening of a condition beyond its compensated state.assess suspected decompensation
action plan
Individualized instructions about monitoring and responding to changes.review the action plan
contact pathway
Defined route for reaching the appropriate care service.distinguish contact pathways
self-monitoring
Person's observation or recording of agreed health information.explain self-monitoring
follow-up interval
Planned time between reviews in the individual's care.confirm the follow-up interval
care-plan discrepancy
Difference between versions or understandings of the intended care plan.resolve a care-plan discrepancy
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Routine messaging and urgent care are different routes. Their actual use must be explained for the patient's setting rather than inferred from a worksheet.

C Notice the language

Ask the patient to explain the plan, not perform obedience

Teach-back evaluates the clarity of the explanation. A misunderstanding is an opportunity to explain differently, not evidence of poor intelligence or unwillingness.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Check the explanation
I want to check that I explained the two routes clearly.
Invite teach-back
Using your plan, tell me which route you would use for each listed situation.
Remove blame
That tells me I need to explain the distinction differently.
Keep it individual
The thresholds and instructions must be the ones agreed for you.
Separate routes
A routine message is not the same as urgent clinical contact.
Verify the list
Let us confirm which medicine list is current.
Resolve a mismatch
These two versions need checking before you rely on them.
Close with clarity
Please show me where the next review and contact details are recorded.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
I explained
Places responsibility for clear communication on the explanation rather than blaming the listener.
Using your plan
Supports teach-back with the actual written information rather than testing unsupported memory.
The ones agreed for you
Prevents individualized thresholds from being treated as universal instructions.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which teach-back request is best?

Answer and explanation

B. Using your plan, explain which contact route applies to each listed situation.

Explaining the appropriate contacts reveals understanding of the plan instead of merely obtaining a yes-or-no answer.

2. Which response avoids blame?

Answer and explanation

C. I need to explain that distinction differently.

Teach-back should prompt clearer explanation rather than criticism of the patient.

3. What must not be invented?

Answer and explanation

A. A universal dose-adjustment threshold

The exercise supplies no numerical or medicine-change instructions to generalize.

4. What does receipt of the sheet establish?

Answer and explanation

C. That Lee has the sheet, not that every instruction is understood

Possession of a document is different from understanding or completion of care.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Teach-back checks the explanation

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Evans: Cardiologist / Lee: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • follow-up interval
  • Orthopnea
  • self-monitoring
  • teach-back
  • care-plan discrepancy
  • diuretic
  • daily weight record
  • action plan
  • contact pathway
  • fluid restriction
  1. Dr Evans
    You have the written plan. Before you leave, let us check how clearly I explained its different parts.
  2. Lee
    I have read the 1, but the contact numbers still confuse me.
  3. Dr Evans
    Thank you for saying so. Tell me which part is unclear, and we can work through that distinction.
  4. Lee
    I cannot tell which 2 is for ordinary questions and which is for something requiring urgent help.
  5. Dr Evans
    We need to make that explicit using your actual instructions. A routine message is not the urgent-care route.
  6. Lee
    I also want to understand what I am observing, not just copy numbers into a 3.
  7. Dr Evans
    We will explain the monitoring purpose and the instructions agreed for you. This discussion does not supply a universal threshold.
  8. Lee
    Good. I have seen different advice about a 4, and I did not know whether it applied to everyone.
  9. Dr Evans
    It does not automatically apply in the same way to everyone. Use the individualized instructions from your treating team.
  10. Lee
    The medicine sheet also needs checking. I do not want to assume an old 5 instruction remains current.
  11. Dr Evans
    Exactly. We will verify the current list rather than let two different versions sit beside each other unexplained.
  12. Lee
    That sounds like a 6 we should resolve before I use the sheet at home.
  13. Dr Evans
    Yes. Now, using the corrected plan, explain how you would choose the contact route for each situation it lists.
  14. Lee
    I can use the written information for that 7 plan rather than guessing which changes matter.
  15. Dr Evans
    Correct. This 8 checks my explanation. A misunderstanding tells me to explain differently, not to blame you.
  16. Lee
    I appreciate that. Some symptoms, such as breathlessness when lying flat, are hard to describe with the usual shorthand.
  17. Dr Evans
    We can use plain words. 9 is the clinical term for that symptom, but your own description remains important.
  18. Lee
    I also need to know when the next review is and who answers questions before it.
  19. Dr Evans
    We will confirm the 10 and the relevant contact details in the actual plan.
  20. Lee
    Then I can describe the monitoring, medicines, and contact routes separately instead of treating the page as one long instruction.

Answers and explanations
  1. action plan Turn 2. An action plan provides individualized monitoring and response instructions, but receiving it does not prove complete understanding.
  2. contact pathway Turn 4. A contact pathway identifies the appropriate route to a service; routine questions and urgent concerns use different actual arrangements.
  3. daily weight record Turn 6. A daily weight record is an agreed monitoring log and does not itself supply a universal threshold for action.
  4. fluid restriction Turn 8. A fluid restriction is an individual clinical instruction when indicated, not a rule that can be borrowed from another person's plan.
  5. diuretic Turn 10. A diuretic is a medicine that increases urine production; its actual instructions need verification rather than guesswork.
  6. care-plan discrepancy Turn 12. A care-plan discrepancy is a difference between versions or understandings that requires verification before reliance.
  7. self-monitoring Turn 14. Self-monitoring means observing or recording agreed information and should be linked to the actual individualized response plan.
  8. teach-back Turn 15. Teach-back checks how clearly the explanation was understood and should prompt clarification rather than blame.
  9. Orthopnea Turn 17. Orthopnea is breathlessness when lying flat; explaining the term does not replace assessment of the person's actual symptoms.
  10. follow-up interval Turn 19. The follow-up interval is the individually planned time between reviews and must not be guessed from a generic worksheet.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Evans: You have the written plan. Before you leave, let us check how clearly I explained its different parts.
  2. Lee: I have read the action plan, but the contact numbers still confuse me.
  3. Dr Evans: Thank you for saying so. Tell me which part is unclear, and we can work through that distinction.
  4. Lee: I cannot tell which contact pathway is for ordinary questions and which is for something requiring urgent help.
  5. Dr Evans: We need to make that explicit using your actual instructions. A routine message is not the urgent-care route.
  6. Lee: I also want to understand what I am observing, not just copy numbers into a daily weight record.
  7. Dr Evans: We will explain the monitoring purpose and the instructions agreed for you. This discussion does not supply a universal threshold.
  8. Lee: Good. I have seen different advice about a fluid restriction, and I did not know whether it applied to everyone.
  9. Dr Evans: It does not automatically apply in the same way to everyone. Use the individualized instructions from your treating team.
  10. Lee: The medicine sheet also needs checking. I do not want to assume an old diuretic instruction remains current.
  11. Dr Evans: Exactly. We will verify the current list rather than let two different versions sit beside each other unexplained.
  12. Lee: That sounds like a care-plan discrepancy we should resolve before I use the sheet at home.
  13. Dr Evans: Yes. Now, using the corrected plan, explain how you would choose the contact route for each situation it lists.
  14. Lee: I can use the written information for that self-monitoring plan rather than guessing which changes matter.
  15. Dr Evans: Correct. This teach-back checks my explanation. A misunderstanding tells me to explain differently, not to blame you.
  16. Lee: I appreciate that. Some symptoms, such as breathlessness when lying flat, are hard to describe with the usual shorthand.
  17. Dr Evans: We can use plain words. Orthopnea is the clinical term for that symptom, but your own description remains important.
  18. Lee: I also need to know when the next review is and who answers questions before it.
  19. Dr Evans: We will confirm the follow-up interval and the relevant contact details in the actual plan.
  20. Lee: Then I can describe the monitoring, medicines, and contact routes separately instead of treating the page as one long instruction.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A home record needs the actual plan

A patient asks how to use monitoring information.

  1. Patient: I have started a home record, but I do not know which changes the team wants me to report.
  2. Cardiologist: The reporting instructions must come from your actual individualized care plan.
  3. Patient: I should not borrow a numerical threshold from another patient's leaflet.
  4. Cardiologist: Correct. A general example is not automatically the instruction appropriate to you.
  5. Patient: Could we review what is recorded, when, and through which contact route?
  6. Cardiologist: Yes. The real plan should distinguish routine information from concerns requiring prompt assessment.
  7. Patient: I also want to know who reviews the record rather than assume it is constantly watched.
  8. Cardiologist: We should explain the actual review arrangements and their limits.
  9. Patient: Then recording information will support care without becoming a substitute for the clinical instructions.
  10. Cardiologist: Exactly. A usable record needs a clear purpose, verified plan, and responsible contact.
A practical barrier is not a character judgment

A patient explains difficulty following a home-care instruction.

  1. Cardiologist: Which part of the agreed home plan has been difficult to carry out?
  2. Patient: The timing conflicts with my work, and I have not managed it consistently.
  3. Cardiologist: Thank you for explaining the barrier rather than letting the record imply everything is straightforward.
  4. Patient: I worry that difficulty will be labeled noncompliance without the practical reason.
  5. Cardiologist: We should describe what happened and review the actual plan with you.
  6. Patient: I do not want to invent my own medicine adjustment to solve the scheduling problem.
  7. Cardiologist: Any change needs the appropriate clinical review rather than an improvised substitution.
  8. Patient: Could we identify what flexibility, if any, is actually available?
  9. Cardiologist: Yes. We should verify the options rather than promise a change before assessment.
  10. Patient: That would make the next discussion specific and useful instead of simply repeating the instruction.
Routine messages and urgent concerns differ

A clinician checks the patient's understanding of contact routes.

  1. Cardiologist: Please explain how you understand the difference between routine follow-up and an urgent concern.
  2. Patient: The routine inbox is for the matters specified in my plan, not a guarantee of immediate assessment.
  3. Cardiologist: That is important. The actual urgent-care instructions must remain separate.
  4. Patient: I should not wait for a routine reply if the real plan says to seek urgent help.
  5. Cardiologist: Correct. A submitted message does not establish that a clinician has reviewed it.
  6. Patient: Could we check the contact details together rather than rely on an old leaflet?
  7. Cardiologist: We should verify the current service information and who responds through each route.
  8. Patient: I also need language support for understanding the written plan.
  9. Cardiologist: We can arrange the appropriate support and check the explanation again.
  10. Patient: Then the plan can be practical rather than merely a document I possess.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Teach-back checks the explanation
  1. Read the corrected plan review. Give a clear pause between routine questions and urgent concerns.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the teach-back request and the non-blaming clarification without adding clinical thresholds.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Two versions of a plan

Kai has an old medicine list and a newly printed plan. Their instructions differ. Nurse Jo will obtain clinician verification. No adjustment is authorized by this exercise.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Kai: "The instructions on the two versions ___."

Answer and explanation

B. differ

The discrepancy concerns conflicting instructions rather than a confirmed current plan.

2. Jo: "We need clinician ___."

Answer and explanation

C. verification

The responsible clinical review must establish which instructions apply.

3. Kai: "This exercise authorizes no medicine ___."

Answer and explanation

C. adjustment

No medicine change may be inferred from the language-practice scenario.

4. Jo: "The current plan remains to be ___."

Answer and explanation

C. clarified

The supplied next step is resolving the discrepancy before relying on a version.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
