Original fictional case: Dr Evans reviews an individualized heart-failure plan with Lee. The actual plan includes symptom monitoring, a verified medicine list, contact routes, and follow-up. No weight threshold, fluid limit, or dose adjustment is supplied here. Lee has received the sheet but is unclear which number is for routine questions and which route is for urgent concerns. The clinician must clarify the distinction and use teach-back without blame.
Language workshop: Teach-back checks the explanation
Communication goal: Check understanding of an individualized plan without treating a printed sheet as proof of comprehension.
Language guidance: A written action plan does not prove understanding. Clarify the actual instructions; the lesson must not become a universal weight, fluid, or medicine-adjustment protocol.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I want to check that I explained the two routes clearly. / Using your plan, tell me which route you would use for each listed situation. / That tells me I need to explain the distinction differently. / The thresholds and instructions must be the ones agreed for you. / A routine message is not the same as urgent clinical contact. / Let us confirm which medicine list is current. / These two versions need checking before you rely on them. / Please show me where the next review and contact details are recorded. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: heart failure: Clinical syndrome in which cardiac dysfunction causes symptoms and signs in context. / congestion: Fluid accumulation associated with relevant circulatory dysfunction. / edema: Swelling caused by fluid in tissues. / orthopnea: Breathlessness occurring when lying flat. / paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea: Episodes of breathlessness waking a person from sleep. / exercise tolerance: Ability to perform physical activity without limiting symptoms. / daily weight record: Log of body-weight measurements under an agreed monitoring plan. / fluid balance: Relationship between fluid intake, output, and retention. / fluid restriction: Individual instruction limiting fluid intake when clinically indicated. / sodium intake: Amount of sodium consumed through food and drink. / diuretic: Medicine increasing urine production. / guideline-directed therapy: Treatment consistent with applicable evidence-based recommendations and individual circumstances. / decompensation: Worsening of a condition beyond its compensated state. / action plan: Individualized instructions about monitoring and responding to changes. / contact pathway: Defined route for reaching the appropriate care service. / self-monitoring: Person's observation or recording of agreed health information. / follow-up interval: Planned time between reviews in the individual's care. / care-plan discrepancy: Difference between versions or understandings of the intended care plan. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected plan review. Give a clear pause between routine questions and urgent concerns. Swap roles. Repeat the teach-back request and the non-blaming clarification without adding clinical thresholds. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Evans: Cardiologist / Lee: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Kai has an old medicine list and a newly printed plan. Their instructions differ. Nurse Jo will obtain clinician verification. No adjustment is authorized by this exercise.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Evans: We can use plain words. Orthopnea is the clinical term for that symptom, but your own description remains important.
Lee: I also need to know when the next review is and who answers questions before it.
Dr Evans: We will confirm the follow-up interval and the relevant contact details in the actual plan.
Lee: Then I can describe the monitoring, medicines, and contact routes separately instead of treating the page as one long instruction.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

07Explaining absolute and relative riskTwenty-five percent of what?+

Your goal

Translate a relative change into comparable absolute frequencies without turning group data into a personal guarantee.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Twenty-five percent of what?

For arithmetic practice only, a fictional comparison describes 40 events among 1,000 people without an intervention and 30 among 1,000 with it over the same five-year period. These are not real treatment estimates or a recommendation. Dr Ali explains the ten-event difference, the one-percentage-point absolute reduction, and the 25 percent relative reduction to Robin. No individual risk estimate is supplied.

Robin: Patient in a fictional numerical exercise / Dr Ali: Cardiologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many events occur in the fictional comparison group?

Answer and explanation

B. 40 per 1,000

The supplied comparison group has forty events among one thousand people over five years.

2. How many events occur in the intervention group?

Answer and explanation

D. 30 per 1,000

The intervention group has thirty events among the same denominator of one thousand.

3. What is the common time period?

Answer and explanation

B. Five years

Both fictional figures refer to the same five-year period for a valid comparison.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Forty per 1,000 is 4 percent; thirty per 1,000 is 3 percent. The difference is one percentage point, equivalent to a 25 percent relative reduction from 4 percent.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
absolute risk
Probability of a defined outcome over a stated time in a specified group.communicate absolute risk
relative risk
Ratio of risk in one group to risk in a comparison group.calculate relative risk
absolute risk reduction
Difference between comparison and intervention risks.explain absolute risk reduction
relative risk reduction
Absolute reduction divided by the comparison risk.explain relative risk reduction
percentage point
Unit for the arithmetic difference between two percentages.distinguish percentage points
denominator
Total number underlying a proportion or rate.state the denominator
natural frequency
Count expressed out of a stated group size.use natural frequencies
time horizon
Period over which an outcome probability is described.specify the time horizon
comparison group
Group providing a reference for evaluating another group.identify the comparison group
intervention group
Group receiving the approach being evaluated.describe the intervention group
event rate
Frequency of specified events in a defined group or observation period.compare event rates
baseline risk
Risk before or without the evaluated change in the relevant comparison.explain baseline risk
confidence interval
Range expressing statistical uncertainty around an estimate under its assumptions.interpret a confidence interval
number needed to treat
Reciprocal of absolute risk reduction for a defined outcome and period.qualify number needed to treat
population estimate
Estimate describing a defined group rather than a guaranteed individual outcome.interpret a population estimate
risk communication
Explanation of probabilities, uncertainty, and possible consequences.improve risk communication
benefit framing
Way of presenting a possible favorable outcome or reduction in harm.balance benefit framing
uncertainty range
Range communicating limits of an estimate's precision or applicability.explain the uncertainty range
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

The figures are invented for language and arithmetic practice. They are not a treatment effect, a personal risk prediction, or evidence for choosing an intervention. Do not invent a confidence interval.

C Notice the language

Keep the denominator visible

A large relative percentage can be heard as a large personal probability change. Use the same group size and time period when comparing figures, then explain the limits of individual prediction.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the denominator
Both figures are out of one thousand people.
Keep time consistent
Both cover the same five-year period.
Use natural frequencies
The comparison is forty events versus thirty.
Explain the difference
That is ten fewer events per thousand.
Name the absolute change
The absolute reduction is one percentage point.
Name the relative change
Ten is one quarter of forty, so the relative reduction is twenty-five percent.
Limit the meaning
These fictional group figures do not predict your individual outcome.
Keep uncertainty honest
No confidence interval has been supplied.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Percent versus percentage point
A change from four percent to three percent is one percentage point, not twenty-five percentage points.
Out of
Introduces the denominator in an accessible natural-frequency comparison.
Over five years
Keeps the same observation period attached to both figures.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. What is the absolute difference?

Answer and explanation

C. 10 fewer events per 1,000

Subtracting thirty from forty gives ten fewer events among one thousand people.

2. What is the absolute percentage-point reduction?

Answer and explanation

B. One percentage point

Four percent minus three percent equals one percentage point.

3. What is the relative reduction?

Answer and explanation

A. 25 percent

The reduction of ten divided by the comparison count of forty is one quarter, or twenty-five percent.

4. Which conclusion must be avoided?

Answer and explanation

A. These figures guarantee Robin's personal outcome.

Fictional group data cannot establish a personal risk or guarantee an individual result.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Twenty-five percent of what?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Robin: Patient in a fictional numerical exercise / Dr Ali: Cardiologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • time horizon
  • population estimate
  • natural frequencies
  • absolute risk reduction
  • percentage point
  • benefit framing
  • Relative risk
  • denominator
  • relative risk reduction
  • confidence interval
  1. Robin
    A twenty-five percent reduction sounds large. Does it mean twenty-five fewer people out of every hundred?
  2. Dr Ali
    Not in this example. We need the 1 and the original risk before interpreting a relative percentage.
  3. Robin
    Please use the counts first. I follow those more easily than a headline percentage on its own.
  4. Dr Ali
    The 2 are forty events per thousand without the intervention and thirty per thousand with it.
  5. Robin
    Are those counted over the same period, or is one an annual number and the other a lifetime number?
  6. Dr Ali
    The 3 is five years for both. We must keep that period attached to the comparison.
  7. Robin
    Then the difference is ten events per thousand, rather than two hundred and fifty per thousand.
  8. Dr Ali
    Correct. The 4 is ten per thousand, which is one percentage point.
  9. Robin
    I want to get the wording right: four percent compared with three percent is one percentage point apart.
  10. Dr Ali
    Exactly. A 5 is the unit for subtracting percentages, not the same as a relative percent change.
  11. Robin
    Where does twenty-five percent come from if the absolute difference is only one percentage point?
  12. Dr Ali
    The 6 is ten divided by forty. The reduction is one quarter of the comparison count.
  13. Robin
    So the relative risk itself is thirty divided by forty, or three quarters of the comparison risk?
  14. Dr Ali
    Yes. 7 is 0.75 in this fictional example; the relative reduction is 0.25, or twenty-five percent.
  15. Robin
    Could someone use only the larger-sounding relative reduction to make the option appear more impressive?
  16. Dr Ali
    That is why 8 matters. Presenting the absolute figures too helps keep the size of the comparison clear.
  17. Robin
    I also understand that these made-up numbers cannot tell me what will happen to me personally.
  18. Dr Ali
    Correct. A 9 would still need careful interpretation. These made-up figures only illustrate the arithmetic; they are not a prediction for you.
  19. Robin
    And we should not add a precision range unless the source actually supplies one and explains it.
  20. Dr Ali
    Exactly. No 10 is supplied here. We should keep that limitation explicit rather than manufacture certainty.

Answers and explanations
  1. denominator Turn 2. The denominator is the group size underlying each figure; both fictional groups contain one thousand people.
  2. natural frequencies Turn 4. Natural frequencies express the counts out of a common group size, here forty versus thirty events per thousand.
  3. time horizon Turn 6. The time horizon is the period over which the event figures apply; both use the same five years.
  4. absolute risk reduction Turn 8. Absolute risk reduction is the difference between the two group risks, here ten per thousand or one percentage point.
  5. percentage point Turn 10. A percentage point measures the difference between percentage values; four percent minus three percent equals one point.
  6. relative risk reduction Turn 12. Relative risk reduction compares the decrease with the original comparison risk; ten divided by forty equals twenty-five percent.
  7. Relative risk Turn 14. Relative risk is the intervention risk divided by the comparison risk, here thirty over forty or 0.75.
  8. benefit framing Turn 16. Benefit framing is how a favorable effect is presented; balanced explanation includes absolute as well as relative figures.
  9. population estimate Turn 18. A population estimate describes a group and does not guarantee the outcome for an individual person.
  10. confidence interval Turn 20. A confidence interval expresses statistical uncertainty under defined assumptions and cannot be invented when no interval is supplied.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Robin: A twenty-five percent reduction sounds large. Does it mean twenty-five fewer people out of every hundred?
  2. Dr Ali: Not in this example. We need the denominator and the original risk before interpreting a relative percentage.
  3. Robin: Please use the counts first. I follow those more easily than a headline percentage on its own.
  4. Dr Ali: The natural frequencies are forty events per thousand without the intervention and thirty per thousand with it.
  5. Robin: Are those counted over the same period, or is one an annual number and the other a lifetime number?
  6. Dr Ali: The time horizon is five years for both. We must keep that period attached to the comparison.
  7. Robin: Then the difference is ten events per thousand, rather than two hundred and fifty per thousand.
  8. Dr Ali: Correct. The absolute risk reduction is ten per thousand, which is one percentage point.
  9. Robin: I want to get the wording right: four percent compared with three percent is one percentage point apart.
  10. Dr Ali: Exactly. A percentage point is the unit for subtracting percentages, not the same as a relative percent change.
  11. Robin: Where does twenty-five percent come from if the absolute difference is only one percentage point?
  12. Dr Ali: The relative risk reduction is ten divided by forty. The reduction is one quarter of the comparison count.
  13. Robin: So the relative risk itself is thirty divided by forty, or three quarters of the comparison risk?
  14. Dr Ali: Yes. Relative risk is 0.75 in this fictional example; the relative reduction is 0.25, or twenty-five percent.
  15. Robin: Could someone use only the larger-sounding relative reduction to make the option appear more impressive?
  16. Dr Ali: That is why benefit framing matters. Presenting the absolute figures too helps keep the size of the comparison clear.
  17. Robin: I also understand that these made-up numbers cannot tell me what will happen to me personally.
  18. Dr Ali: Correct. A population estimate would still need careful interpretation. These made-up figures only illustrate the arithmetic; they are not a prediction for you.
  19. Robin: And we should not add a precision range unless the source actually supplies one and explains it.
  20. Dr Ali: Exactly. No confidence interval is supplied here. We should keep that limitation explicit rather than manufacture certainty.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The denominator changes the meaning

A clinician checks a fictional numerical comparison.

  1. Cardiologist: In this fictional example, the same outcome occurs in forty versus thirty people per thousand over five years.
  2. Patient: So the absolute difference is ten people per thousand over that period.
  3. Cardiologist: Correct. Both groups use the same outcome, denominator, and timeframe.
  4. Patient: That is four percent versus three percent, a one-percentage-point absolute reduction.
  5. Cardiologist: Yes. The relative reduction is twenty-five percent compared with the original forty.
  6. Patient: I can see why saying twenty-five percent alone might sound larger than the absolute change.
  7. Cardiologist: Both descriptions can be correct, but they answer different comparison questions.
  8. Patient: These are teaching figures, not evidence about a medicine I should take.
  9. Cardiologist: Exactly. No personal treatment recommendation follows from the fictional arithmetic.
  10. Patient: A clear explanation should state the numbers and their context rather than only the most impressive percentage.
Relative change stays the same while absolute change differs

A patient works through a second fictional comparison.

  1. Patient: In another fictional example, the outcome changes from twenty to fifteen people per thousand.
  2. Cardiologist: The absolute difference is five per thousand, provided the outcome and timeframe match.
  3. Patient: That is two percent versus one and a half percent.
  4. Cardiologist: Yes, an absolute reduction of half a percentage point.
  5. Patient: The relative reduction is still twenty-five percent because five is a quarter of twenty.
  6. Cardiologist: Correct. The relative percentage matches the earlier example, but the absolute reduction is smaller.
  7. Patient: So an identical relative reduction does not imply an identical number of people affected.
  8. Cardiologist: Exactly. The starting frequency matters when explaining what the difference means.
  9. Patient: We should not transfer these invented figures to a real person's prognosis.
  10. Cardiologist: They are language and arithmetic practice, not a clinical risk estimate.
A percentage needs a named outcome

Two clinicians revise an unclear risk statement.

  1. Clinician: The draft says risk falls by thirty percent, but it does not name the outcome or period.
  2. Cardiologist: That statement is incomplete. We need the actual outcome, baseline frequency, comparison, and timeframe.
  3. Clinician: It also fails to say whether thirty percent is relative or absolute.
  4. Cardiologist: That distinction can change the listener's understanding substantially.
  5. Clinician: We should not fill the missing numbers from memory merely to make the sentence sound finished.
  6. Cardiologist: Correct. Verify the source and explain its uncertainty and relevance to the actual discussion.
  7. Clinician: If the data are unavailable, the limitation should be stated rather than hidden.
  8. Cardiologist: Yes. A precise-looking percentage is not useful when the comparison remains undefined.
  9. Clinician: The revised explanation will use verified figures and comparable denominators where available.
  10. Cardiologist: Then the patient can understand the claim instead of being persuaded by an isolated number.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Twenty-five percent of what?
  1. Read the corrected numerical explanation. Stress per thousand, over five years, and percentage point.
  2. Swap roles. Say the absolute and relative differences using only the fictional figures supplied.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A second fictional comparison

For arithmetic practice only, fictional five-year event counts are 20 per 1,000 in one group and 15 per 1,000 in another. No treatment recommendation is implied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Clinician: "The absolute difference is ___ per thousand."

Answer and explanation

C. five

Twenty minus fifteen gives five events per thousand in this fictional comparison.

2. Patient: "That is ___ percentage points."

Answer and explanation

B. 0.5

Five divided by one thousand is half of one percent, or 0.5 percentage points.

3. Clinician: "The relative reduction is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

A. twenty-five

Five divided by the comparison count of twenty equals twenty-five percent.

4. Patient: "These numbers remain ___."

Answer and explanation

D. fictional

The figures are invented for practice and do not support a clinical recommendation.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Coordinating rehabilitation and return to activityReferred is not yet enrolled+

Your goal

Explain a rehabilitation referral and negotiate practical follow-up without giving an unsupplied exercise prescription.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Referred is not yet enrolled

Dr Reed has referred Sam to cardiac rehabilitation after an assessed cardiac event. The rehabilitation team has received the referral, but no intake appointment is confirmed. Sam wants to return to a physically demanding job and has a transport barrier. Exercise clearance, work restrictions, and an individual program require the actual clinical assessment; none is supplied in this exercise. Coordinator Inez will help clarify access arrangements.

Sam: Patient / Dr Reed: Cardiologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is confirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. Receipt of the rehabilitation referral

The receiving team has the referral, but later assessment and booking stages remain unresolved.

2. What barrier does Sam report?

Answer and explanation

A. Transport

Transport is the stated practical obstacle, not a lack of interest.

3. What requires individual assessment?

Answer and explanation

B. Exercise and work guidance

The exercise supplies no clearance or program from which to give activity instructions.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A rehabilitation referral is not an exercise prescription or work-clearance certificate. Keep acceptance, intake, assessment, and participation distinct.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
cardiac rehabilitation
Supervised program supporting recovery, risk reduction, and functioning after eligible cardiac conditions.explain cardiac rehabilitation
rehabilitation intake
Initial assessment and planning encounter for a rehabilitation program.arrange rehabilitation intake
exercise prescription
Individualized specification of appropriate exercise under clinical assessment.review an exercise prescription
functional capacity
Ability to perform physical tasks under relevant conditions.assess functional capacity
graded activity
Activity progressed in steps under an appropriate plan.discuss graded activity
supervised exercise
Exercise performed with the relevant professional oversight.arrange supervised exercise
return-to-work assessment
Evaluation of readiness and requirements for resuming work.request a return-to-work assessment
work restriction
Specified limitation on work activity under an appropriate assessment.clarify work restrictions
occupational demand
Physical or other requirement of a person's job.describe occupational demands
secondary prevention
Measures reducing recurrence or complications after established disease.discuss secondary prevention
risk-factor modification
Changes directed at relevant disease risk factors.support risk-factor modification
rehabilitation goal
Individual objective for recovery or functioning.set a rehabilitation goal
attendance barrier
Obstacle to taking part in scheduled care.identify an attendance barrier
transport arrangement
Plan for travel to and from a service.confirm transport arrangements
program eligibility
Whether a person meets requirements for a particular service.verify program eligibility
coverage verification
Checking the applicable financial or insurance arrangements.obtain coverage verification
intake confirmation
Verification that an initial program appointment is booked.obtain intake confirmation
care coordinator
Professional organizing connected services and practical arrangements.contact the care coordinator
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Do not promise coverage, transport, or a place in a program before verification. Help identify the responsible person and the unresolved practical questions.

C Notice the language

Practical barriers are part of the clinical conversation

Attendance can be difficult even when the patient wants support. Ask about transport and work demands before labeling missed access as a lack of motivation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Explain the service
Rehabilitation combines assessed activity, education, and support.
State the stage
The team has received the referral; intake is not yet confirmed.
Ask about access
What would make getting to the sessions difficult?
Describe the job
Tell us what the work requires physically.
Avoid clearance by implication
This referral is not a statement that every work task is safe for you.
Name the coordinator
Inez can help clarify the access arrangements.
Separate coverage
We need to verify the applicable coverage rather than promise it.
Confirm the next step
Let us identify who will confirm the intake and contact you.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Received versus confirmed
Receipt concerns the referral; confirmed intake concerns a booked appointment.
Requires physically
Elicits actual job demands instead of relying on a job title alone.
Can help clarify
Offers coordination without guaranteeing a service or financial outcome.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement preserves the referral stage?

Answer and explanation

A. Received, but intake not yet confirmed.

The brief confirms receipt only and leaves intake and individual assessment unfinished.

2. Which question addresses access respectfully?

Answer and explanation

B. What makes travel to the sessions difficult?

Asking what makes travel difficult explores the stated barrier without inventing a negative attitude.

3. What must not be inferred from the referral?

Answer and explanation

A. A safe exercise intensity or unrestricted work clearance

Actual exercise and work guidance requires the appropriate individual clinical assessment.

4. Which promise is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Every cost and journey will definitely be covered.

The case supplies no verified financial or transport entitlement to guarantee.

Referred is not yet enrolled

Sam: Patient / Dr Reed: Cardiologist

Answers and explanations
  1. occupational demands Turn 2. Occupational demands are the actual job tasks relevant to the work assessment.
  2. return-to-work assessment Turn 4. A return-to-work assessment evaluates actual readiness; rehabilitation attendance alone does not establish it.
  3. Cardiac rehabilitation Turn 6. Cardiac rehabilitation is supervised care, not automatic authorization for unrestricted activity.
  4. intake confirmation Turn 8. Intake confirmation establishes the receiving program's actual appointment, not merely referral receipt.
  5. attendance barrier Turn 10. An attendance barrier is a practical obstacle, not necessarily refusal of rehabilitation.
  6. coverage verification Turn 12. Coverage verification checks the actual financial arrangements and prevents an unsupported promise that every cost is covered.
  7. exercise prescription Turn 14. An exercise prescription specifies activity for an individual after appropriate assessment and should not be copied from another person's program.
  8. rehabilitation goal Turn 16. A rehabilitation goal is an individualized recovery or functioning objective that connects the program with the person's needs.
  9. graded activity Turn 18. Graded activity progresses in planned steps under an appropriate individual plan rather than an unsupplied universal schedule.
  10. care coordinator Turn 20. A care coordinator connects services and practical arrangements while clinical decisions remain with the appropriately responsible professionals.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sam: I want to return to work, but the job involves lifting and long periods on my feet.
  2. Dr Reed: Those occupational demands need to be described for the assessment rather than inferred from your job title.
  3. Sam: Does the rehabilitation referral mean I have already been cleared to do every part of the job?
  4. Dr Reed: No. A return-to-work assessment is a separate clinical question. The referral is not unrestricted clearance.
  5. Sam: Then what is rehabilitation intended to add beyond another appointment about the event itself?
  6. Dr Reed: Cardiac rehabilitation can combine assessed activity, education, and support, with a program suited to the relevant clinical circumstances.
  7. Sam: I received a letter saying the referral arrived. I assumed that meant I had a first session booked.
  8. Dr Reed: The team has received it, but intake confirmation has not yet been provided. We need to make that next step clear.
  9. Sam: Getting there may be difficult. I do not have a reliable lift during the hours the service usually operates.
  10. Dr Reed: That attendance barrier matters. Inez can help clarify transport and access arrangements rather than assume you do not want to attend.
  11. Sam: I also need to know whether the sessions are covered before I commit to costs I cannot manage.
  12. Dr Reed: We need coverage verification. I cannot promise a financial outcome without checking the applicable arrangements.
  13. Sam: A friend suggested copying their exercise schedule while I wait. Is that the same as the program I will receive?
  14. Dr Reed: No. An exercise prescription needs your own assessment. Ask the rehabilitation team what is appropriate for you rather than copying your friend's schedule.
  15. Sam: I would like the program to help me with the tasks that actually matter at home and work.
  16. Dr Reed: That is a useful rehabilitation goal to discuss with the team as they assess your circumstances.
  17. Sam: I understand that progress may involve different steps rather than an immediate return to everything.
  18. Dr Reed: Yes. Any graded activity must follow the agreed clinical plan, with the relevant review and support.
  19. Sam: For now I need the intake date, transport information, and a clear route for my work questions.
  20. Dr Reed: We will identify the care coordinator and responsible clinicians for those questions, without treating the referral as a completed assessment.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A rehabilitation referral is not an intake visit

A patient asks whether a program has accepted the referral.

  1. Patient: You sent a rehabilitation referral, so I assumed my first visit had already been arranged.
  2. Cardiologist: Sending the referral does not confirm receipt, acceptance, or an appointment.
  3. Patient: Could we check which stage is actually complete rather than leave me waiting without a contact?
  4. Cardiologist: Yes. We should identify the receiving service and the route for checking progress.
  5. Patient: I also want to know what information the program still needs.
  6. Cardiologist: That should be clarified through the actual intake process, not guessed from a generic checklist.
  7. Patient: Does the referral itself give me an exercise prescription?
  8. Cardiologist: No. The appropriate clinical assessment and program plan determine the actual activity instructions.
  9. Patient: Then I should distinguish the referral from clearance or a training schedule.
  10. Cardiologist: Exactly. We will explain the verified next step without implying that assessment has already happened.
Transport affects attendance

A rehabilitation coordinator discusses an access barrier.

  1. Coordinator: The program has contacted you about intake. Is there a practical issue we should know about?
  2. Patient: Transport is difficult, and I am worried that missing a visit will look like refusing rehabilitation.
  3. Coordinator: We should record the access barrier accurately rather than assume your intention.
  4. Patient: I want to attend, but I need to understand the actual scheduling and travel options.
  5. Coordinator: We can check what is available without promising a service before confirmation.
  6. Patient: Could you also clarify which contact handles questions about coverage?
  7. Coordinator: Yes. Coverage questions need the appropriate team and your actual arrangements.
  8. Patient: I do not want an estimate presented as a guarantee that every visit is paid for.
  9. Coordinator: We will distinguish the estimate, any authorization, and confirmed coverage.
  10. Patient: That helps me plan without turning the practical difficulty into a judgment about my motivation.
Returning to work requires a real assessment

A patient asks whether rehabilitation attendance proves work readiness.

  1. Patient: If I attend rehabilitation, does that automatically mean I am cleared for every task at work?
  2. Cardiologist: No. Participation and work readiness are different questions requiring the actual assessment.
  3. Patient: My job includes duties that may differ from what another patient does.
  4. Cardiologist: The actual job demands matter, along with your clinical situation and relevant professional review.
  5. Patient: Could a letter describe a restriction that has not been assessed just to satisfy a form?
  6. Cardiologist: It should not. Any clinical statement needs an appropriate factual basis.
  7. Patient: I would like the employer process kept separate from a promise about a medical outcome.
  8. Cardiologist: That distinction is important, including what information is authorized for sharing.
  9. Patient: Then rehabilitation can support recovery without becoming a universal work-clearance certificate.
  10. Cardiologist: Exactly. The record should state what is established and which decision still needs the appropriate review.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Referred is not yet enrolled
  1. Read the corrected rehabilitation discussion. Contrast referral receipt, intake, and work assessment.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the transport-barrier question without implying poor motivation or promising coverage.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A job title hides the task

Rory works in a warehouse and asks about returning to lifting. The clinical team requests the actual task demands before assessing restrictions. No lifting limit is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Rory: "My job includes ___."

Answer and explanation

D. lifting

Lifting is the actual work activity raised in the scenario.

2. Clinician: "We need the task ___ before assessing restrictions."

Answer and explanation

A. demands

A job title alone does not specify the relevant physical requirements.

3. Rory: "No lifting limit has been ___."

Answer and explanation

B. supplied

The exercise provides no numerical or clinical restriction to infer.

4. Clinician: "The work guidance needs individual ___."

Answer and explanation

D. assessment

The appropriate clinical assessment must precede individualized work guidance.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

REFERENCE
Course: Cardiology English
Professional audience: cardiologists, interventional cardiologists, electrophysiologists, cardiology fellows
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Lesson 08: Coordinating rehabilitation and return to activity
Original fictional case: Dr Reed has referred Sam to cardiac rehabilitation after an assessed cardiac event. The rehabilitation team has received the referral, but no intake appointment is confirmed. Sam wants to return to a physically demanding job and has a transport barrier. Exercise clearance, work restrictions, and an individual program require the actual clinical assessment; none is supplied in this exercise. Coordinator Inez will help clarify access arrangements.
Language workshop: Referred is not yet enrolled
Communication goal: Explain a rehabilitation referral and negotiate practical follow-up without giving an unsupplied exercise prescription.
Language guidance: A rehabilitation referral is not an exercise prescription or work-clearance certificate. Keep acceptance, intake, assessment, and participation distinct.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Rehabilitation combines assessed activity, education, and support. / The team has received the referral; intake is not yet confirmed. / What would make getting to the sessions difficult? / Tell us what the work requires physically. / This referral is not a statement that every work task is safe for you. / Inez can help clarify the access arrangements. / We need to verify the applicable coverage rather than promise it. / Let us identify who will confirm the intake and contact you. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: cardiac rehabilitation: Supervised program supporting recovery, risk reduction, and functioning after eligible cardiac conditions. / rehabilitation intake: Initial assessment and planning encounter for a rehabilitation program. / exercise prescription: Individualized specification of appropriate exercise under clinical assessment. / functional capacity: Ability to perform physical tasks under relevant conditions. / graded activity: Activity progressed in steps under an appropriate plan. / supervised exercise: Exercise performed with the relevant professional oversight. / return-to-work assessment: Evaluation of readiness and requirements for resuming work. / work restriction: Specified limitation on work activity under an appropriate assessment. / occupational demand: Physical or other requirement of a person's job. / secondary prevention: Measures reducing recurrence or complications after established disease. / risk-factor modification: Changes directed at relevant disease risk factors. / rehabilitation goal: Individual objective for recovery or functioning. / attendance barrier: Obstacle to taking part in scheduled care. / transport arrangement: Plan for travel to and from a service. / program eligibility: Whether a person meets requirements for a particular service. / coverage verification: Checking the applicable financial or insurance arrangements. / intake confirmation: Verification that an initial program appointment is booked. / care coordinator: Professional organizing connected services and practical arrangements. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected rehabilitation discussion. Contrast referral receipt, intake, and work assessment. Swap roles. Repeat the transport-barrier question without implying poor motivation or promising coverage. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Sam: Patient / Dr Reed: Cardiologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Rory works in a warehouse and asks about returning to lifting. The clinical team requests the actual task demands before assessing restrictions. No lifting limit is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sam: I understand that progress may involve different steps rather than an immediate return to everything.
Dr Reed: Yes. Any graded activity must follow the agreed clinical plan, with the relevant review and support.
Sam: For now I need the intake date, transport information, and a clear route for my work questions.
Dr Reed: We will identify the care coordinator and responsible clinicians for those questions, without treating the referral as a completed assessment.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

An alert from a cardiac device

Distinguish a device transmission, an alert, and a reviewed clinical finding.

Patient Ellis sees a message that a device transmission was received and asks whether it proves a new problem. Dr Kim explains the actual review process. No alert type, device setting, symptom assessment, or diagnosis is supplied. Device messaging is not a substitute for the actual urgent-care route if concerning symptoms occur.

Ellis: Patient / Dr Kim: Cardiologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • review workflow
  • device transmission
  • review status
  • urgent-care instructions
  • alert
  • Data availability
  1. Ellis
    My device account says a transmission was received. Does that mean the team has found a new heart problem?
  2. Dr Kim
    Receipt confirms that information arrived. It does not, by itself, tell us the clinical interpretation.
  3. Ellis
    I thought every notification meant a clinician had already reviewed the recording and reached a conclusion.
  4. Dr Kim
    A 1 and a reviewed finding are separate stages.
  5. Ellis
    What about the word alert? Is that always a diagnosis rather than a signal for someone to check?
  6. Dr Kim
    An 2 needs the actual system context and review; the word alone does not establish a diagnosis.
  7. Ellis
    I do not want to ignore something important, but I also do not want to invent a conclusion.
  8. Dr Kim
    We should clarify the real 3 and who communicates the outcome, without promising a finding before review.
  9. Ellis
    Does this kind of system always mean someone is watching every heartbeat in real time?
  10. Dr Kim
    Do not assume that. The service must explain the actual monitoring arrangements and their limitations for your device.
  11. Ellis
    If I have a concerning new symptom, should I just send data and wait for the next message?
  12. Dr Kim
    Follow the actual 4 rather than treating a routine transmission as a substitute for assessment.
  13. Ellis
    I also want to know how to report a problem with the connection itself.
  14. Dr Kim
    The team can explain the appropriate technical contact and distinguish a connection problem from a clinical finding.
  15. Ellis
    So a missing signal would not automatically prove that my heart rhythm is normal or abnormal?
  16. Dr Kim
    Correct. 5 is a separate question from what the information means clinically.
  17. Ellis
    Could we confirm who explains the reviewed result and how I can check the status?
  18. Dr Kim
    Yes. We will identify the actual contact route and distinguish a pending review from a completed discussion.
  19. Ellis
    That helps me understand the message without mistaking an administrative confirmation for a diagnosis.
  20. Dr Kim
    A clear 6 prevents exactly that confusion while keeping the appropriate clinical contact route visible.

Answers and explanations
  1. device transmission Turn 4. A device transmission is information sent from the monitoring system and is not automatically a reviewed clinical finding.
  2. alert Turn 6. An alert flags information for attention under the actual system and requires context rather than automatically establishing a diagnosis.
  3. review workflow Turn 8. The review workflow describes how the service receives, assesses, and communicates device information rather than assuming every notification has already been interpreted.
  4. urgent-care instructions Turn 12. Urgent-care instructions identify the appropriate clinical response to concerning symptoms and are not replaced by sending a routine device transmission.
  5. Data availability Turn 16. Data availability concerns whether usable information reached the service and cannot itself establish the underlying clinical state when data are missing.
  6. review status Turn 20. Review status identifies whether information is awaiting assessment or has been reviewed and should not be inferred solely from a receipt notification.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ellis: My device account says a transmission was received. Does that mean the team has found a new heart problem?
  2. Dr Kim: Receipt confirms that information arrived. It does not, by itself, tell us the clinical interpretation.
  3. Ellis: I thought every notification meant a clinician had already reviewed the recording and reached a conclusion.
  4. Dr Kim: A device transmission and a reviewed finding are separate stages.
  5. Ellis: What about the word alert? Is that always a diagnosis rather than a signal for someone to check?
  6. Dr Kim: An alert needs the actual system context and review; the word alone does not establish a diagnosis.
  7. Ellis: I do not want to ignore something important, but I also do not want to invent a conclusion.
  8. Dr Kim: We should clarify the real review workflow and who communicates the outcome, without promising a finding before review.
  9. Ellis: Does this kind of system always mean someone is watching every heartbeat in real time?
  10. Dr Kim: Do not assume that. The service must explain the actual monitoring arrangements and their limitations for your device.
  11. Ellis: If I have a concerning new symptom, should I just send data and wait for the next message?
  12. Dr Kim: Follow the actual urgent-care instructions rather than treating a routine transmission as a substitute for assessment.
  13. Ellis: I also want to know how to report a problem with the connection itself.
  14. Dr Kim: The team can explain the appropriate technical contact and distinguish a connection problem from a clinical finding.
  15. Ellis: So a missing signal would not automatically prove that my heart rhythm is normal or abnormal?
  16. Dr Kim: Correct. Data availability is a separate question from what the information means clinically.
  17. Ellis: Could we confirm who explains the reviewed result and how I can check the status?
  18. Dr Kim: Yes. We will identify the actual contact route and distinguish a pending review from a completed discussion.
  19. Ellis: That helps me understand the message without mistaking an administrative confirmation for a diagnosis.
  20. Dr Kim: A clear review status prevents exactly that confusion while keeping the appropriate clinical contact route visible.
The data arrived but review is pending

The service confirms receipt of a transmission. Clinical review is pending. The patient has not reported a symptom in this update.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Patient: "The transmission was ___."

Answer and explanation

C. received

The service confirms that the device information arrived.

2. Clinician: "Clinical review remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. pending

Receipt does not mean the clinical interpretation is complete.

3. Patient: "That is not a confirmed ___."

Answer and explanation

A. diagnosis

A received transmission alone does not establish a clinical diagnosis.

4. Clinician: "We should clarify the review ___."

Answer and explanation

A. status

The relevant question is the actual stage of review and communication.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

This scenario in the book, page 76 ↗
Reconciling a cross-specialty medicine plan

Resolve conflicting instructions through the responsible clinicians without independently changing treatment.

Dr Reed, a cardiologist, and Dr Hall, a procedural clinician, find conflicting medicine instructions in two appointment documents. The actual medicines, procedure, and doses are not supplied. They agree to verify the current plan, responsible prescriber, and patient communication before presenting an instruction as settled.

Dr Reed: Cardiologist / Dr Hall: Procedural clinician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • responsible prescriber
  • document version
  • reconciled instruction
  • procedural context
  • communication ownership
  • closed-loop reconciliation
  1. Dr Hall
    Our appointment document contains a medicine instruction that conflicts with the cardiology letter. The patient has noticed the difference.
  2. Dr Reed
    We need to resolve that through the responsible clinicians, not ask the patient to guess which document to follow.
  3. Dr Hall
    I will identify the exact instruction and date rather than summarize it as a general disagreement.
  4. Dr Reed
    The 1 matters. We should establish which copies are current and what authority each instruction has.
  5. Dr Hall
    The procedure team needs the actual clinical context, not an assumption that all procedures use identical instructions.
  6. Dr Reed
    Agreed. The 2 must be verified before a medicine plan is treated as appropriate.
  7. Dr Hall
    We should also identify the clinician responsible for the prescription rather than assume the appointment office owns the decision.
  8. Dr Reed
    Let us confirm the 3 and the necessary cross-specialty discussion.
  9. Dr Hall
    Until then, I will not describe a stop or restart instruction as agreed merely because it appears in one letter.
  10. Dr Reed
    Correct. We are reconciling conflicting information, not issuing a new medicine instruction in this conversation.
  11. Dr Hall
    The patient needs a single clear explanation once the actual decision has been verified.
  12. Dr Reed
    A 4 should identify the relevant medicine details, timing, and contact route in the real clinical plan.
  13. Dr Hall
    If one document is superseded, should we retain the correction history rather than silently erase the old wording?
  14. Dr Reed
    Yes. Follow the actual record-correction process so the change and its authority remain traceable.
  15. Dr Hall
    I will confirm who contacts the patient and how we verify that the conflicting message has been corrected.
  16. Dr Reed
    That is 5, which must not disappear after the clinicians agree among themselves.
  17. Dr Hall
    We should also update the relevant teams so the old instruction is not repeated at the next appointment.
  18. Dr Reed
    Exactly. A resolved discussion is incomplete if the outdated message remains the one everyone keeps using.
  19. Dr Hall
    I will record the verified plan, the responsible clinicians, and the communication outcome under our actual process.
  20. Dr Reed
    Then 6 includes both a clinical decision and accurate communication to the people who need it.

Answers and explanations
  1. document version Turn 4. The document version identifies which dated instruction is being discussed and helps distinguish a superseded copy from a current plan.
  2. procedural context Turn 6. Procedural context includes the actual planned intervention and relevant circumstances, which must be known before medicine instructions are reconciled.
  3. responsible prescriber Turn 8. The responsible prescriber is the clinician accountable for the relevant medicine decision and should be identified explicitly when instructions conflict.
  4. reconciled instruction Turn 12. A reconciled instruction is the verified direction after conflicting information has been resolved by the appropriate clinicians.
  5. communication ownership Turn 16. Communication ownership assigns responsibility for explaining the verified plan and correcting the earlier conflicting message to the patient.
  6. closed-loop reconciliation Turn 20. Closed-loop reconciliation resolves the conflicting instructions and verifies that the appropriate recipients have the updated plan rather than merely documenting internal agreement.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Hall: Our appointment document contains a medicine instruction that conflicts with the cardiology letter. The patient has noticed the difference.
  2. Dr Reed: We need to resolve that through the responsible clinicians, not ask the patient to guess which document to follow.
  3. Dr Hall: I will identify the exact instruction and date rather than summarize it as a general disagreement.
  4. Dr Reed: The document version matters. We should establish which copies are current and what authority each instruction has.
  5. Dr Hall: The procedure team needs the actual clinical context, not an assumption that all procedures use identical instructions.
  6. Dr Reed: Agreed. The procedural context must be verified before a medicine plan is treated as appropriate.
  7. Dr Hall: We should also identify the clinician responsible for the prescription rather than assume the appointment office owns the decision.
  8. Dr Reed: Let us confirm the responsible prescriber and the necessary cross-specialty discussion.
  9. Dr Hall: Until then, I will not describe a stop or restart instruction as agreed merely because it appears in one letter.
  10. Dr Reed: Correct. We are reconciling conflicting information, not issuing a new medicine instruction in this conversation.
  11. Dr Hall: The patient needs a single clear explanation once the actual decision has been verified.
  12. Dr Reed: A reconciled instruction should identify the relevant medicine details, timing, and contact route in the real clinical plan.
  13. Dr Hall: If one document is superseded, should we retain the correction history rather than silently erase the old wording?
  14. Dr Reed: Yes. Follow the actual record-correction process so the change and its authority remain traceable.
  15. Dr Hall: I will confirm who contacts the patient and how we verify that the conflicting message has been corrected.
  16. Dr Reed: That is communication ownership, which must not disappear after the clinicians agree among themselves.

  18. Dr Reed: Exactly. A resolved discussion is incomplete if the outdated message remains the one everyone keeps using.

The old letter is still being used

The clinicians have verified the current plan, but an appointment office is still sending the older letter. The team requests an authorized correction and patient communication.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Clinician: "The current plan is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. verified

The clinical plan has been checked and agreed by the relevant clinicians.

2. Office: "We are still sending the ___ letter."

Answer and explanation

D. older

The remaining problem is distribution of the superseded document.

3. Clinician: "We need an authorized ___."

Answer and explanation

A. correction

The outdated communication must be corrected through the proper record and document process.

4. Office: "The patient also needs clear ___."

Answer and explanation

A. communication

An internal correction alone does not ensure the patient receives the accurate plan.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
